#798. Germany's far-right AfD forges a pro-Russia alliance with the BSW

In Saxony-Anhalt, the AfD and BSW are joining forces to push for negotiations with Moscow. What is driving this rapprochement?

The state parliament of the eastern state of Saxony-Anhalt is calling for an immediate end to all German financial and military aid to Ukraine. The motion was passed during a special parliamentary session with the votes of the far-right populist Alternative for Germany (AfD) and the Alliance for Social Justice and Economic Reason (BSW) which combines left-leaning economic positions with right-wing populist positions on immigration. Both parties are urging Saxony-Anhalt's current state premier Sven Schulze, of the center-right Christian Democratic Union (CDU), to advocate in the Bundesrat, Germany's upper house representing the federal states, for peace negotiations with Russia.

However, as the federal states have no specific powers in the field of foreign policy, the motion will have no direct impact on Germany's Ukraine policy.

Speaking on behalf of the BSW in the state parliament, legislator Claudia Wittig called on the premier to take action: "It is your responsibility to finally use every available avenue to work for peace."

The proposal drew sharp criticism from all other parties. "Anyone who demands a ceasefire only from the side that has been attacked is not calling for peace, but for capitulation," said CDU lawmaker Stefan Ruland.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

The special session took place against the backdrop of ongoing negotiations over the formation of a new state government in Saxony-Anhalt. The AfD emerged as the clear winner of the September 2026 election, securing just under 44% of the vote. However, it fell short of an absolute majority in the state parliament and will need the support of other parties if it is to govern.

The BSW, which narrowly secured representation in the state parliament, is the only party willing to hold formal talks with the AfD on forming a government. Because the AfD is classified by the security authorities in the eastern German state as a "confirmed right-wing extremist" party, all other parties have ruled out any cooperation.

Observers view the joint motion by the AfD and BSW as a sign of closer and potentially enduring cooperation between two parties rooted in very different ideological traditions.

"Their common ground lies in their rejection of the political establishment, their pro-Putin stance, and their criticism of Germany's support for Ukraine," political scientist Armin Pfahl-Traughber of the Federal University of Applied Administrative Sciences said in an interview with DW.

The BSW was founded in 2024 as a breakaway from the socialist Left Party. Its founder, Sahra Wagenknecht, after whom the alliance was originally named, had long been a controversial figure on the German left and advocated far-reaching changes to Germany's economic policy. She was the Left Party's most prominent representative of the so-called "Communist Platform", which maintained close ties to Russia.

Over time, however, her socially conservative positions and criticism of Germany's immigration policy came under increasing scrutiny within the party. These growing tensions ultimately led to her departure from the Left Party and the founding of the BSW.

The AfD, too, has repeatedly expressed sympathy for Putin's system. Shortly after the start of Russia's war against Ukraine, Björn Höcke, the influential AfD state chairman in Thuringia, described Russia as a "champion of a world of free and sovereign states."

The growing rapprochement between the two parties made headlines following a meeting in early October. At the invitation of a far-right publishing house, representatives of the AfD and BSW met in Saxony-Anhalt for what was described as a confidence-building dinner. Also in attendance were controversial figures from both the far right and the dogmatic left. According to their own accounts, participants from both sides explored the possibility of closer cooperation in a cordial atmosphere and in a spirit of mutual trust.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Jürgen Elsässer, the host of the event and editor-in-chief of the right-wing magazine Compact, has for years promoted the idea of a so-called "cross-front" alliance (Querfront) between the far left and the far right in opposition to Germany's political establishment. He has long sought to build bridges between the two camps.

Pfahl-Traughber does not regard the cooperation between the AfD and BSW as a "cross-front" alliance: "I am not even sure that the BSW can still be classified as part of the political left." While the party's founder, Wagenknecht, has her roots on the left, she and her allies position themselves on the economic left but the cultural right, the political scientist argued.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

The prominent German historian and author Ilko-Sascha Kowalczuk likewise does not consider the BSW as a left-wing party. In an interview with DW, he argued: "The BSW was never left-wing; it was always reactionary."

Kowalczuk considers both the AfD and the BSW as threats to democracy: "The BSW and the AfD are two sides of the same coin. Both are mouthpieces for the Kremlin, both pursue similar domestic political objectives, and both seek a comparable anti-democratic transformation of the state into an authoritarian system."

Although the recent resolution passed by the Saxony-Anhalt state parliament is unlikely to have any immediate impact on Germany's policy towards Ukraine, Kowalczuk warns of its broader implications: "For now, the AfD and BSW remain isolated, but with positions like these they are appealing to large sections of society, particularly in eastern Germany. The erosion of solidarity with Ukraine will continue and will soon spread to larger segments of both the SPD and the CDU."

This article was originally published in German.

#797. Bruno’s betrayal? ‘Toxic’ Fernandes is symptom of United’s culture of entitlement | Jamie Jackson

Michael Carrick must now deal with more dressing-room unrest after Lisandro Martínez’s attack on the board

“Toxic”: this is the damning characterisation of Bruno Fernandes from one highly placed Manchester United source after the captain’s incendiary betrayal of Michael Carrick.

There has surely never been a greater anti-leadership act in United’s history than Fernandes questioning the man who trusts him to unify the dressing room and execute his on-field plan. The Portuguese has proved this faith wrong at the precise moment he was required to step up and exude a defiant togetherness.

Nothing could be more dysfunctional than civil war between United’s head coach and captain, featuring Carrick biting back at Fernandes for questioning his tactics as matters hit a new low in the aftermath of Wednesday’s pathetic 4-1 loss to Como.

A litmus test for any leader comes when the storm arrives. At the Giuseppe Sinigaglia Stadium, Fernandes failed miserably as he squealed about “the staff” deciding the best tactic was not to press Cesc Fàbregas’s bright, emerging team.

For “staff” read Carrick because he decides strategy and bears responsibility for performance. Fernandes is Carrick’s on-field proxy, so his behaviour is the dictionary definition of betrayal. His conduct is not expected to lead to an disciplinary action in the way Lisandro Martínez’s calling out last week of Jason Wilcox as, perhaps, this is a greyer area.

Discussing tactics may be more within Fernandes’s remit, as captain, than a defender suggesting the director of football’s recruitment failed. Provided there is no damage to the team. But even if Fernandes issues an apology, some acts are irreversible. There is a case for Carrick to strip the 32-year-old of the captaincy and appoint a senior man such as Youri Tielemans or Harry Maguire.

Carrick is expected to hold his pre-game media conference on Friday afternoon and be in charge for Bournemouth’s visit on Sunday, so when the team is announced whether Fernandes has the armband will intrigue. Could he even drop the playmaker? Whatever the decision, Fernandes can have zero excuse for his Como outburst.

Fernandes witnessed how Martínez’s criticism of Wilcox became an extra headache for Carrick, whose sole focus should have been on arresting the direst start to a campaign since 1986. Instead, Carrick had to thrash out the most prudent line of action – Martínez is understood to have been fined – then field questions about the episode.

Yet still Fernandes rounded on Carrick after one of United’s poorest displays in a European competition. It was a performance the player was part of, having been on the field until Carrick removed him on 67 minutes. Being replaced by Joshua Zirkzee, who United tried to offload in the last window, shows how ineffective Fernandes was, first as a false 9, then, after the interval, in his favoured No 10 role.

Fernandes’s comments indicate he is seriously disaffected with team strategy and his role within this. But is there, too, a frustration at still being at the club as his prime years – he was 32 last month – begin to expire? This is someone who voiced disquiet last December, during a jarring interview with Canal 11, the Portuguese Football Federation‑owned channel, at United’s desire to sell him.

Fernandes was asked whether there had been an offer from Paris Saint-Germain or Real Madrid. He said: “Nowadays, the issue of loyalty is no longer seen the way it used to be. I could have left in the last window and would have earned a lot more.

“At one point I was going to leave and I would have won many trophies that season. I decided to stay not only for family reasons, but because I genuinely like the club. The conversation with the coach [Ruben Amorim] also made me stay.

“But from the club’s side, I felt: ‘If you go, it’s not really that bad for us.’ More than hurting, it makes me sad, because I’m a player they have nothing to criticise me for.”

Kick off your evenings with the Guardian's take on the world of football

Now the club, and specifically Carrick, do feel let down by Fernandes. But, it is also relevant to ask how Fernandes (and Martínez) have come to feel so at ease with going off-message.

These are highly remunerated employees. Their job is to play and maintain a united front – whatever happens – and for Carrick to allow a culture of entitlement to fester shows up a serious fault in his management. For now, Carrick and Fernandes limp on – just like the team.

It will be some act for Carrick to save his job – even victory over Bournemouth may not do so – and for Fernandes to rehabilitate himself, before the head coach and fans. But this is football, a world that can be unpredictable and illogical.

The measure, though, of the debacle at Como, and the wider malaise at the club, can be found in Fàbregas’s post-victory words when he bemoaned allowing Zirkzee’s added-time strike for United.

“I regret the last 10 minutes because I was the one who told the team to slow down the tempo because there’s another match [Sassuolo, on Saturday] and at 4-0 we could manage things a little,” he said.

“Their goal hurt me because we dropped our intensity and dropped our defensive block, but it was my fault.”

How Carrick wishes this was what he had to contend with rather than a mutinous captain and a ship that seems to be sinking deeper on every matchday.

The ticket ban covers two Champions League away games, Uefa confirmed.

The ruling means Liverpool will play January’s match with only their own fans inside the stadium as they aim to qualify for the Champions League knockout stages.

Andoni Iraola’s men have taken maximum points from their opening three fixtures and are third in the Champions League standings behind PSG and Aston Villa.

The Reds have also found form in the Premier League, having only lost one of their first eight matches in England’s top-flight this season.

Their final game before the November international break will be a trip to Crystal Palace on 8 November.

#792. Too much RNA can starve cells of energy, scientists discover

Scientists have uncovered a surprising way that viruses can interfere with the body's cells. When too much RNA accumulates inside a cell, it can damage the structures that produce energy, potentially leaving the cell struggling to function normally.

A new study led by researchers at the Texas A&M College of Veterinary Medicine and Biomedical Sciences (VMBS) found that excess RNA produced during poxvirus infections can impair mitochondria, the tiny structures responsible for generating most of the energy cells need to survive.

Published in Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences, the research reveals an unexpected connection between RNA buildup and cellular energy production. The findings could have implications far beyond viral infections, potentially helping scientists better understand aging-related diseases and improve RNA-based medical treatments, including mRNA vaccines.

"Scientists have long known that RNA degradation helps control protein production and remove defective RNA," said Dr. Zhilong Yang, a professor in VMBS' Department of Veterinary Pathobiology. "Our study reveals another important role: it helps cells maintain the energy they need to function properly."

Cells constantly produce and break down RNA as part of their normal activities. This cleanup process, known as RNA degradation, helps regulate protein production, eliminate faulty RNA, and control immune responses. The new findings suggest it also performs another essential function by protecting the cell's energy supply.

RNA plays several important roles in keeping cells functioning. Messenger RNA (mRNA), for example, carries genetic instructions that tell cells which proteins to produce. These proteins are needed for nearly everything cells do, from maintaining their structure to carrying out essential chemical reactions.

Another type, double-stranded RNA (dsRNA), frequently appears during viral infections. Its presence can serve as a warning signal, alerting the immune system that a virus may have entered the cell.

Viruses often generate large quantities of RNA as they reproduce inside infected cells. Ordinarily, cellular cleanup systems help prevent RNA from accumulating to harmful levels. However, when RNA production exceeds the cell's ability to break it down, the excess material can interfere with mitochondria and reduce energy production.

Scientists already understood that excessive double-stranded RNA could provoke potentially harmful immune responses. What surprised the researchers was discovering that too much ordinary messenger RNA could also damage mitochondria.

"MRNA is a normal component of our cells, but it seems like with many good things, if we get too much, that can become a problem for the cell," Yang said. "When too much RNA builds up, including mRNA, it can damage the mitochondria and interfere with the cell's ability to produce energy."

This creates an unusual challenge for viruses. Although viruses depend entirely on the cells they infect to supply energy and manufacture proteins, producing excessive amounts of viral RNA can damage the very machinery they need to reproduce.

According to Yang, poxviruses, a group that includes the viruses responsible for smallpox and mpox, appear to have ways of managing this problem. They use RNA cleanup mechanisms to remove excess RNA, helping infected cells remain functional long enough for viral replication to continue.

"If the cell is not healthy, the virus would not replicate well," Yang said. "The virus needs to keep RNA levels balanced inside the cell."

Scientists Discover RNA Can Directly Damage Mitochondria

One of the study's most unexpected findings emerged when researchers investigated exactly how excess RNA affects mitochondria.

Initially, the team suspected that RNA buildup was damaging these cellular powerhouses indirectly by activating the immune system. Certain types of RNA are known to trigger defensive responses, which can place additional stress on infected cells.

However, experiments revealed that the damage did not require an immune response.

"When we isolated only the mitochondria and added RNA, we still saw mitochondrial damage," said Dr. Djamal Brahim Belhaouari, the study's first author. "That was the moment we realized this could be a previously unknown cellular response to RNA."

The researchers also observed that mitochondrial problems developed before substantial immune activation occurred. Furthermore, the damage did not depend on the immune pathways normally activated during infection.

Together, these results indicate that excess RNA itself can interfere with mitochondrial function, rather than simply causing damage through the body's response to a virus.

The precise mechanism remains unclear, but the researchers have proposed a possible explanation involving electrical charges.

Mitochondria produce energy by maintaining an electrical and chemical difference across their inner membranes. This difference helps drive the production of ATP, the molecule cells use as their primary energy source.

RNA carries a negative electrical charge. The researchers suspect that when unusually large amounts accumulate near mitochondria, they may disturb the electrical conditions required for efficient energy production.

"We think the negatively charged RNA may accumulate around mitochondria and disrupt the electrical balance needed for energy production, although how it happens is unknown," Yang said. "This is a new concept that we are continuing to explore."

What the Discovery Could Mean for Aging and Disease

Although the experiments focused on viral infections, the findings raise questions about whether RNA buildup might contribute to other health problems.

Abnormal RNA accumulation has been observed in several conditions, including certain cancers, neurodegenerative diseases, and disorders associated with aging. Neurodegenerative diseases involve the progressive deterioration of nerve cells, as occurs in conditions such as Alzheimer's and Parkinson's disease.

Because mitochondria are essential for maintaining healthy cells, disruptions to their energy production could potentially contribute to cellular dysfunction in some of these conditions.

The researchers suggest that excess RNA may represent one previously unrecognized source of mitochondrial stress. However, further studies are needed to determine how broadly this mechanism operates and whether it directly contributes to these diseases.

"We think this has quite broad translational implications," Brahim Belhaouari said. "Too much double-stranded RNA or mRNA that cannot be degraded effectively may contribute to disease processes in some contexts."

Potential Implications for mRNA Vaccines and Future Treatments

The discovery could also provide useful information for scientists developing medicines that rely on RNA.

RNA-based therapies, including mRNA vaccines, work by introducing RNA instructions into cells to produce specific proteins. Understanding how cells respond to RNA, process it, and eventually break it down is important for designing these treatments.

By identifying another way excessive RNA can affect cellular health, the findings may help researchers optimize future RNA-based therapies and vaccines. The study does not establish that existing mRNA vaccines cause mitochondrial damage in people.

"Understanding this mechanism could help researchers design and optimize future RNA-based therapeutics and vaccines by better understanding how cells respond to large amounts of RNA," Yang said.

More broadly, the research suggests that controlling RNA levels is important for more than maintaining accurate protein production or managing immune activity. It may also be essential for keeping mitochondria functioning and ensuring cells have enough energy.

For the scientists, the most striking result was that even a molecule cells depend on every day can become harmful when it accumulates beyond normal levels.

"Scientists already knew that too much double-stranded RNA can be harmful," Yang said. "What surprised us was finding out that too much mRNA -- something cells normally need every day -- can also damage mitochondria and interfere with the cell's ability to produce energy."

Research reported in this study was supported, in part, by the National Institute of Allergy and Infectious Diseases (R01AI143709 to Z.Y.) and Texas A&M University. The content is solely the responsibility of the authors and does not necessarily represent the official views of the NIH.

Materials provided by Texas A&M University. Original written by Camryn Haines. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#791. Will that do? Lamine Yamal conjures another magical spell as Barça pass first test | Sid Lowe

The 19-year-old scored a cracker in Seville, days after his ‘almost rabona’ at Galatasaray, as La Liga leaders beat Real Betis

When at last it was time for Lamine Yamal to leave, the 19-year-old’s number going up before the 90th minute for only the second time since the opening day, Real Betis fans whistled him all the way to the touchline where Barcelona’s coach was waiting. His work here was done, Hansi Flick invited him to take the weight off his feet and his shoulders, not that he wanted to. First, though, he took Lamine Yamal’s head in his hands and, leaning in away from the amateur lip readers, tried to make himself heard over the noise. “I told him I was proud of him because he played a fantastic game,” he said.

At least he could think of something. When Lamine Yamal had opened the scoring just nine minutes into the match that was supposed to find out how good Barcelona really were, it hadn’t been easy. Nothing erudite, anyway. On the morning that Barcelona went to Betis – first v third, a proper opponent at last – Jorge Valdano had lamented that sometimes he doesn’t have the words, and he always has the words. Valdano had just watched Lamine do that, whatever that was, in Istanbul – an “almost rabona”, the kid called it – and now he had a problem. “If he keeps doing strange things, we’re not going to be able to explain it,” he wrote.

To which Lamine Yamal responded by doing another strange thing that same afternoon, scoring another brilliant goal to set Barcelona on the way to a 5-2 win over Betis in a game that made history. And if Lamine Yamal had been a little lucky three days before, momentarily losing control only for the rebound to bounce back to him like the ball knew where it would rather be, this was all him and very him. The move started with Gerard Martín losing his boot and ended with pretty much everyone else losing their heads. In Martín’s momentary absence, Rodri dropped into defence and began it from there, the ball moved across the box and back until it was played up to Lamine Yamal. His superb pass with the outside of the boot unlocked everything and then, receiving again just inside Betis’s half, he set off.

There’s a moment, Lamine Yamal says, when you can sense the anticipation, feel people getting up one after the other all along the stand, a wave accompanying you, moving with you. At that moment, he says, there’s some inner power and you feel like Superman or Spider-Man; the defenders are robots, he says, and however good a robot is, however well programmed to stop him, a human is better. Too fast, too agile, too inspired. Too much thought gone into this, too much psychology. Man against the machines, code this. He rolled the ball one way with his left foot, flicked it the other with the heel of his right, and from the edge of the box, sent it flying into the far top corner.

Somehow. Sometimes you have nothing new to say, you know? It’s not big and it’s not clever but sometimes only swearing will do: sometimes only holy shit! hits the spot. Sometimes you can be sitting alone on the sofa and still shout: “Oh, my God!”, call them in to watch this. But it won’t be the same when they do, even if it’s sort of better: a chance to slow it down and break it down, see the detail, work out how he did it, like you’re ever going to do that. Because for all the expectation, it’s still a shock seen live, the purest reaction of all.

Words? Pah! This weekend, Gabriel Jesus put it best: as the ball passed him, he just held his head in his hands. Lamine Yamal ran to the corner. Fermín López chased, wiping his brow: woah, what was that? Karim Adeyemi went wagging his fingers. On the touchline Flick almost took off his assistant’s hand with the hardest high five. Teammates ran towards him not so much celebrating as laughing, so good it’s funny and even funnier for being repeated, an outrageous goal his catchphrase. Lamine Yamal sat alongside Jules Koundé on the advertising board, arms crossed like it was easy, like “what are you lot losing your heads about?” Maybe it is. And there is something in the way he hit it that felt a little like he put it in the top corner because, well, why not? Might as well.

Lamine Yamal got up, stood holding the corner flag, looking about: will that do? If this was the first test Barcelona have faced, it hadn’t taken him 10 minutes to score again, and like that. And that’s the thing. Who knows, this might not even have been the best goal of the weekend: there were glorious moments from Arda Guler and Kylian Mbappé in Real Madrid’s win against Sevilla. Javi Rodríguez’s run was wonderful for Celta Vigo against Alavés. And Pablo Martínez’s shot in Málaga’s 2-5 loss to Real Sociedad was ridiculous. It might not have even been the best shot of this game had Gio Lo Celso’s “goal” not been ruled out. And it definitely might not even be the best Lamine Yamal has scored, but this keeps happening. Statement after statement.

He didn’t stop there either: there was an absurd pass from which Adeyemi should have scored. A run through three. Another assist. And the moment that got the Betis fans whistling him: when, with López on the ground and Lamine Yamal going over, Facundo Bernal pushed him, he took a run-up and pushed Bernal back, and the defender head-butted him, seeing red. The game hadn’t stopped with his first either; instead, Rodri said afterwards, it had “everything”. Always watch Betis, especially when they’re playing Barcelona: unbeaten in 15 home games, the last time anyone beat them here was Barça too. And it finished 5-3. This time they were only one short.

Barcelona had dominated the first 25 minutes, the ball theirs alone but Valentín Gómez headed in to make it 1-1 before Rodri made it 2-1. Within two minutes, Lo Celso had smashed in an extraordinary 25-yard shot on the bounce and the turn to make it 2-2, only for it to be ruled out for a handball 14 seconds earlier. All that before half-time. And yet it was summed up better by the 30 seconds that passed between Álvaro Valles making a superb save at one end and Joan García matching it at the other; by the 59 seconds that passed between that and Jesus getting the third. Or the two minutes 59 seconds between that going in and Abdessamad Ezzalzouli making it 3-2. Then the fight that followed it, Lamine Yamal tumbling maybe a touch too hard and Bernal walking.

By the time Lamine Yamal did, at last encouraged to rest, Eric García had scored the fourth and it was done. “With this result, we had to make the change,” Flick said; with tensions rising too. Dani Olmo added a neat fifth and it finished. Betis were furious. Asked how it had left him feeling, their manager, Manuel Pellegrini, said: “impotent”. Lo Celso’s goal shouldn’t have been ruled out and Jesus’s should, he said. Ask how he felt, Fran García said “frustrated, angry … and tired”. They had given everything, the ovation as generous as their effort had been. “This is one of the hardest games in La Liga,” Rodri said, but Barcelona had won again.

That’s nine from nine, a new record. Now, a word of advice from Mr Wolf. When it comes to historic starts, Barcelona’s previous record was under Tata Martino, who you may remember but probably don’t. “This is a nice thing but we want to win titles,” Flick said; Martino did not. Betis were the first team Barcelona have played in the top half of the table. The rest currently sit in 19th, 18th, 17th, 16th, 15th 14th, and 11th. Harder games are coming: Paris Saint-Germain next, then Real Madrid. They also have an injury crisis at the back. But no one is playing like them, and certainly not scoring like them. They have scored 39 league goals, 48 in 11 matches in all competitions.

Deportivo A Coruña 1-1 Levante, Espanyol 0-1 Atlético, Villarreal 0-0 Elche, Real Betis 2-5 Barcelona, Valencia 0-0 A Bilbao, Osasuna 2-0 Racing Santander, Celta Vigo 2-1 Alavés, Málaga 2-5 Real Sociedad, Real Madrid 4-1 Sevilla

When Robert Lewandowski, Ferran Torres and Marcus Rashford went in the summer, when they didn’t manage to sign Julián Álvarez, you couldn’t help asking where the goals were going to come from. The answer was everywhere. Thirteen different players have scored. It turns out that they have the best striker in Europe: Raphinha, the man who is just filling in there, has scored more than anyone else. They may also have the best player at 19, the kid who came back a World Cup winner and still wasn’t satisfied.

“After the game against Elche at the start of the season, people were saying: ‘He’s not right,’” Lamine Yamal said. “But I was calm at home, thinking: ‘I’ve just won the World Cup, I’ve not had a break, I know my body: bit by bit I’ll get to the point where I explode and start to score. And those same people will suddenly be saying I’m the best.’” In the first two games, against Elche and Athletic Bilbao, Lamine Yamal did little. Since then he has scored or assisted in every match. Nine goals, 10 assists, and most of them brilliant. He went to the international break and scored after 104 seconds at Wembley, 70 in Seville, and 320 in Oviedo. Here, on the day they said this time it’s for real, it took him 530, heads in hands, words stuck in throats, and another win on the way.

“It was amazing, a match for all the people who love football,” Flick said. “For me Lamine is the best in the world. I’m happy about his performance; this is what I love to see. And when you see the first goal, it’s unbelievable.”

#787. Nord Stream trial opens in Germany, defense seeks dismissal

Serhii Kuznietsov is accused of attacking civilian infrastructure in the 2022 sabotage of the Russian gas pipeline to Germany. His lawyers called Nord Stream a military target, and said their client is sworn to secrecy.

The trial of a former Ukrainian special forces officer, Serhii Kuznietsov, opened in the northern German city of Hamburg on Wednesday amid considerable public and press interest.

Kuznietsov is accused of leading the September 2022 sabotage operation that put the undersea Nord Stream 1 and 2 gas pipelines linking Russian to Germany out of action. The pipelines remain inactive four years later, with no sign of plans to ever reactivate them as Russia's full-scale invasion of Ukraine heads into its fifth winter.

Prosecutors accuse the Ukrainian of participation in a war crime by targeting civilian infrastructure, but his defense lawyers on Wednesday argued that the case should be dismissed entirely on multiple grounds.

A team of three lawyers representing Kuznietsov questioned the premise of the trial in court.

One of Kuznietsov's lawyers, Nicola Canestrini, argued that it was not valid to try the officer on war crimes charges for an attack on civilian infrastructure.

He argued that the pipeline, which provided half of Germany's natural gas imports prior to Russia's 2022 invasion of Ukraine, was ultimately a "Russian weapon," given the importance of energy revenues for funding Moscow's military.

"Is it unlawful to destroy an aggressor's infrastructure without harming any human beings?" Canestrini asked the court.

After the charges were read out, the judge asked Kuznietsov if he wanted to comment, and he said that he did not.

His lawyers argue that this silence extends beyond not speaking in court and say this should be the main grounds for dismissal.

They say Kuznietsov is sworn to secrecy by his home government in Ukraine and so is unable to discuss his case or potential exonerating evidence with anyone, including his legal representatives.

"He is not permitted to speak. His two immediate superiors told him that he does not have authorization to make any statements — not even to his lawyers," defense attorney Jes Meyer-Lohkamp told the court. In German law, Meyer-Lohkamp argued: "Anyone who cannot speak out cannot be convicted."

DW's Matthew Moore was on hand in the court in northern Germany on Wednesday.

There is huge interest in this court case, Moore reported. No sooner had the court doors opened at 07:30 local time in Hamburg than the first journalists, observers and protesters began arriving.

A small pro-Ukraine protest held up a banner saying, "Russia is a terrorist." One participant from Germany told Moore they wanted to remind the world of "the war crimes taking place against the Ukrainian people."

Inside, a Ukrainian student told DW that he and others had made the journey from Kyiv to show support for the defendant, Serhii Kuznietsov.

"Serhii Kuznietsov is a hero," the student said. In the eyes of the German prosecutors, it is another matter. They accuse him of carrying out a war crime. If found guilty of his part in the gas pipeline bombing, Kuznietsov faces a lengthy prison sentence.

The case has been highly politically awkward for Germany — a staunch supporter of Ukraine despite investigators pursuing a trail that led to Kuznietsov and other suspects.

Ukraine's President Volodymyr Zelenskyy has repeatedly denied any Ukrainian involvement in the operation, and had also resisted Kuznietsov's extradition to Germany after his arrest in Italy last year, arguing that there was no case to answer.

German Foreign Minister Johann Wadephul declined to comment on the trial on Wednesday, saying that the most important thing for now was for the case to proceed with no political interference or restrictions.

"We will deal with any outcome, and we will, of course, take it into account in our political calculations," he told a press conference in Berlin.

Court dates for the trial are currently scheduled through February of next year but this could be subject to change in either direction.

With additional reporting from DW's Matthew Moore in Hamburg.

#786. Samsung TVs might use the mics of connected devices to listen to you, researcher says

If you own a Samsung TV without a built-in microphone, it might still be listening to you, according to a researcher who shared his findings this week.

On the social media platform X, cybersecurity researcher @log4j shared that an investigation into Samsung smart TVs found that televisions without a mic will scan for connected devices that are equipped with a microphone. The smart TV will then utilize the audio data collected from the soundbar or other connected Bluetooth devices.

"This is pretty evil and clever," @log4j said about their discovery.

The researcher recently gained administrator root access to their own 2025 Samsung smart TV and has been sharing their findings in a series of posts on X.

Your smart TV is likely listening to you. At this point, that shouldn't be a surprise. Many televisions advertise this functionality, since your TV has to be listening to you to take voice commands and interact with other smart home devices.

However, other unadvertised and concerning privacy issues are arising from the device's ability to listen in.

For example, @log4j started looking into their TV after receiving oddly specific advertisements and found that their television was logging things like "dog barking" and "baby crying" under an event called EV201/SOUNDSENSOR_ON.

As NotebookCheck.net points out, while Samsung 2025 Vision AI TVs advertise that the devices detect barking dogs and crying babies and send alerts through the company's SmartThings app, Samsung does not advertise that this data is also sent back to the company.

Researcher @log4j also found that Samsung TVs track what happens inside specific apps. For example, the TV monitors what a user does in the YouTube app and even logs data such as the ads a user skips.

Mashable has reached out to Samsung for more information about the reports from @log4j and will update this piece when we hear back.

These privacy issues are not unique to Samsung TVs either. Last month, Mashable covered a report from YouTuber Gamers Nexus, which claimed to find that LG TVs were secretly recording users even when the televisions were turned off. LG later responded, denying some but not all the claims.

#782. Private Griffin moon lander gets put to the test | Space photo of the day for Oct. 23, 2026

Join The Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

The robotic Griffin lunar lander has passed a host of critical tests ahead of its first-ever moon mission, which is scheduled to launch later this year.

At NASA's Jet Propulsion Laboratory (JPL) in Southern California, Voyager Technologies' Griffin Mission One (or Griffin-1) lunar lander has gone through an absolute gauntlet of tests. The spacecraft was put through environmental testing that explored how the vehicle will fare in the extreme conditions it will experience on its ride to the surface of the moon.

This testing simulated the extreme temperatures, shaking and even the sounds that the spacecraft will experience from launch to landing. And, as JPL announced recently, the lander passed this series of tests with flying colors, as we can see in this image snapped Aug. 31 of the lander inside the 25-foot-wide (7.6 meters) space simulator at JPL.

"NASA JPL’s Environmental Test Laboratory has been putting robotic explorers to the test since the Ranger program gave us NASA’s first up-close views of the moon's surface in the mid-1960s. We’re proud to provide our expertise and facilities to support this new phase of commercial lunar exploration," Charles Whetsel, manager for the Exploration Systems Development Office at JPL, said in a statement.

Griffin-1 is expected to launch to the moon later this year as part of NASA's CLPS (Commercial Lunar Payload Services) initiative. The spacecraft will carry over 1,100 pounds (455 kilograms) of cargo to its landing site near the lunar south pole.

In this cargo will be science experiments and new technologies that will be tested on the moon's surface, including Astrolab's FLIP rover, which will explore the lunar surface with a history-making supercomputer onboard.

This suite of experimental hardware won't just expand our understanding of the moon up close. Griffin-1's exploration will also help set the stage for a future, crewed moon base, which NASA aims to build near the south pole over the next half-decade or so.

#779. Millions take fish oil for brain health. A two-year study found no benefit

Millions of Americans take fish oil supplements in hopes of protecting their memory and keeping their brains healthy as they age. These products have become a major business, with Americans spending more than $1 billion each year on supplements containing omega-3 fatty acids. These essential nutrients help build and maintain the connections between brain cells that support memory, learning, and other cognitive functions.

But a new study suggests that getting more omega-3s into the brain through supplements may not provide the protection many people expect.

Researchers at Keck Medicine of USC found that even when high doses of omega-3s successfully reached the brain, they did not produce measurable improvements in memory, thinking abilities, or protection against brain shrinkage.

The findings, published in eBioMedicine, come from a two-year clinical trial involving older adults considered to be at increased risk of developing Alzheimer's disease. Despite taking omega-3 supplements daily, participants showed no significant cognitive advantage over those who received a placebo.

"We all wish there was a silver bullet for preventing Alzheimer's, but our findings showed that fish oil supplements do not appear to protect brain health," said Hussein Naji Yassine, MD, director of the USC Center for Personalized Brain Health and lead investigator of the study. "While omega-3s play an important role in forming brain cell connections needed for cognition, our results do not support fish oil supplements as a preventive measure against Alzheimer's."

A Two-Year Study Tests Fish Oil's Brain Benefits

To investigate whether omega-3 supplements could protect the aging brain, researchers enrolled 365 adults between the ages of 55 and 80. All participants rarely ate fish, a major dietary source of omega-3 fatty acids, and were considered at elevated risk for Alzheimer's disease.

Nearly half (47%) carried APOE4, a gene variant associated with a substantially higher risk of developing Alzheimer's later in life. Although having this variant does not mean someone will necessarily develop the disease, it is the strongest known genetic risk factor for late-onset Alzheimer's.

Participants were randomly assigned to receive either a daily fish oil supplement or a placebo, an inactive treatment used for comparison. The supplements provided 2,000 mg of docosahexaenoic acid (DHA), an omega-3 fatty acid that is especially important for the brain.

DHA is a major component of brain cell membranes, helping maintain the structure and function of neurons. It also supports communication between nerve cells, making it an important nutrient for normal brain activity.

The study was double-blind, meaning neither participants nor researchers knew who was receiving the active supplement during the trial. This approach helps reduce bias and makes it easier to determine whether any observed benefits are actually caused by the treatment.

Omega-3 Levels Rose, but Memory Did Not Improve

Before evaluating whether the supplements improved brain health, researchers needed to answer a fundamental question: Were the omega-3 fatty acids actually reaching the brain?

To find out, they measured DHA concentrations in cerebrospinal fluid, the clear liquid that surrounds and cushions the brain and spinal cord. Examining this fluid can provide information about substances entering and circulating within the central nervous system.

After six months, DHA levels in the cerebrospinal fluid had increased by an average of 17% among participants taking the supplements. This provided evidence that the omega-3 fatty acids were reaching their intended destination.

However, getting DHA into the central nervous system was only part of the goal. The more important question was whether those higher levels would translate into better brain health.

Researchers assessed participants' memory and other cognitive abilities at the beginning of the trial and again after two years. Despite the increase in DHA, those taking fish oil performed no better on the cognitive tests than participants receiving the placebo.

Brain imaging revealed similarly disappointing results.

The supplements did not prevent shrinkage of the hippocampus, a region deep within the brain that plays a central role in forming and organizing memories. The hippocampus is particularly vulnerable to changes associated with aging and Alzheimer's disease, making its size an important indicator researchers use when studying brain health.

Taken together, the results suggest that simply increasing DHA availability in the brain is not enough to produce measurable cognitive benefits in this group of older adults.

Why Fish Oil Supplements May Work Differently Than Food

The unexpected findings have raised a new question for researchers: If omega-3s can reach the brain, why don't higher levels necessarily improve its function?

Yassine and his colleagues are now investigating how the brain absorbs, processes, and uses these nutrients. Their earlier research suggests that omega-3s may be more beneficial when consumed as part of a broader dietary pattern rather than taken in isolation as a supplement.

One possibility involves the Mediterranean-style diet, which emphasizes foods such as fish, vegetables, fruits, whole grains, legumes, nuts, and olive oil. This eating pattern naturally supplies omega-3 fatty acids alongside other nutrients and has been associated with a lower risk of Alzheimer's disease.

The researchers suspect that the combination of nutrients in a balanced diet, along with an individual's overall health, may influence how effectively omega-3s support the brain.

"We're focused on better understanding how the brain processes omega-3s and whether factors, such as poor health, dietary pattern, genetic risk and age, may change the brain's ability to effectively absorb and use omega-3s," said Yassine. "We are working to develop medications that may help the brain better utilize these nutrients to preserve cognitive function."

Understanding these differences could eventually help scientists identify why certain people might respond better to omega-3s than others and whether new treatments could improve the brain's ability to benefit from these essential fats.

Importantly, the findings do not mean omega-3 fatty acids are unnecessary or that eating fish offers no health benefits. Instead, they indicate that taking high-dose DHA supplements alone did not provide measurable protection against cognitive decline or hippocampal shrinkage in the population studied.

Healthy Habits Remain Key to Alzheimer's Prevention

Although the trial focused specifically on fish oil supplements, the researchers emphasize that protecting brain health requires a much broader approach.

Rather than relying on a single supplement, they recommend maintaining healthy habits throughout life, including regular physical activity, sufficient high-quality sleep, and a nutritious, balanced diet.

These behaviors support overall health and may help reduce factors associated with cognitive decline. Physical exercise, for example, promotes cardiovascular health and blood flow, while sleep plays an important role in memory formation and normal brain maintenance.

"Staying healthy throughout life remains the most powerful tool we have for reducing Alzheimer's risk, including regular exercise, quality sleep and a balanced diet," said Yassine. "Living a healthy lifestyle is the brain's equivalent of getting regular car maintenance and high-quality oil changes. The brain is more likely to lose greater function if health issues in other parts of the body go unaddressed, in the same way that car engines stop working if regular maintenance is skipped."

While these lifestyle factors were not directly tested in the trial, the researchers believe they offer a more promising foundation for long-term brain health than depending on fish oil supplements alone.

The results also highlight an important distinction: A nutrient can be essential for healthy brain function without providing additional protection when consumed in large doses as a supplement. Understanding why that happens may open new avenues for research into Alzheimer's prevention and the nutritional needs of the aging brain.

The research was supported by the National Institutes of Health, grant numbers RF1AG076124, R01AG055770, R01AG067063, R01AG054434, R21AG056518, P30AG066530 and R01AG054434 and the Alzheimer's Drug Discovery Foundation, grant number GC-201711-2014197.

Materials provided by University of Southern California - Health Sciences. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#778. Brazil election: many scientists relieved as Lula wins surprise victory

Thank you for visiting nature.com. You are using a browser version with limited support for CSS. To obtain the best experience, we recommend you use a more up to date browser (or turn off compatibility mode in Internet Explorer). In the meantime, to ensure continued support, we are displaying the site without styles and JavaScript.

Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva celebrated on the evening of 25 October after coming from behind to win re-election as Brazil’s president.Credit: Maira Erlich/Bloomberg via Getty Images

Brazil’s President Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva won his bid for re-election on 25 October, to the relief of many scientists who feared for the future of Brazilian research if right-wing candidate Flávio Bolsonaro prevailed.

In recent weeks, some of Brazil’s leading scientific organizations, including the Brazilian Academy of Sciences in Rio de Janeiro, circulated an open letter endorsing Lula, a leftist former labour leader. It received more than 20,000 signatures. “It’s hard to express just how happy the scientific community and the Brazilian society is with Lula’s victory,” says Paulo Artaxo, a physicist at the University of São Paulo.

Science philanthropy is rising in Brazil ― upending a long tradition

Science philanthropy is rising in Brazil ― upending a long tradition

Many worried that, if Bolsonaro were elected, he might slash support for Brazilian science and undermine it in the way that his father, Jair Bolsonaro, did. Jair was Brazil’s president from 2019 to 2023, and during that time, he cut science funding and expressed anti-science rhetoric during the COVID-19 pandemic, casting doubt on vaccines and promoting unproven treatments. The former president could not run for office again because he was sentenced to prison over a coup plot that he instigated after he lost his bid for re-election to Lula in 2022. He is currently serving the sentence at home because of health concerns.

It was a surprise victory yesterday for Lula, who won 51.8% of the vote compared with Flávio Bolsonaro’s 48.2%. During the first round of voting on 4 October, Lula finished behind Bolsonaro, but because neither candidate earned 50% of the total votes, a second round of voting was required. “It was very moving to watch this historic turnaround,” says Sidarta Ribeiro, a neuroscientist at the Federal University of Rio Grande do Norte in Natal.

Lula’s victory came at the end of a tumultuous election day. Shortly before the polls were meant to close, a justice in Brazil’s Supreme Court ordered them to remain open for an additional two hours. The move was requested by both Lula’s and Bolsonaro’s campaigns because of public transportation problems that made it difficult for voters to reach polling stations. In a short speech yesterday, Bolsonaro said he would not accept the results.

This will be Lula’s fourth term overall as president of Brazil, a record for the country. He initially led Brazil for two terms between 2003 and 2011. During those terms and his current term, which started in 2023, he championed the expansion of federal universities and efforts to make higher education accessible to a broad range of Brazilians, support that he has pledged to continue. He also launched a program in 2024 that brought back about 600 Brazilian scientists to the country from international institutions, where some had gone seeking improved funding and resources.

“Lula said very clearly in his victory speech that we need to strengthen federal universities and federal institutes and significantly improve how we prepare young people for a new world of [artificial intelligence] and robotics,” Ribeiro says.

Supporters of Brazilian President Lula cheered in the streets of São Paulo on 25 October after it became clear that he had won his bid for re-election in a tight race.Credit: Rodrigo Paiva/Getty

Flávio Bolsonaro had pledged to transfer operation of Brazil’s 69 federal universities from the Ministry of Education to the Ministry of Science and Technology, which has a much smaller budget. In addition, he said he would emphasize applied sciences over basic research, which concerned some in the scientific community because “the countries that have made the greatest advances in science are those with an extremely strong foundation in basic research”, Ribeiro says.

Another major source of concern for some researchers about the election was the future of Brazil’s environmental policies. Under President Lula, deforestation in Brazil’s considerable portion of the Amazon rainforest fell to its lowest level since measurements began in 1988. Between 2019 and 2023, however, deforestation rose by almost 60% under Jair Bolsonaro, and a similar trend was expected under his son. The Amazon rainforest is considered one of the world’s largest carbon sinks, but studies suggest that increasing deforestation could cause it to release more carbon than it absorbs.

Meanwhile, Lula said during his recent campaign that he is committed to ending deforestation in Brazil by 2030, and that he would cut the country’s greenhouse-gas emissions in line with the Paris Agreement, which nations signed onto nearly a decade ago to curb climate change. “Lula’s government plan is very clear: he’ll work towards zero deforestation in the Amazon and towards implementing energy transition programs that end the use of fossil fuels in Brazil,” Artaxo says.

#777. Manchester City, the UAE and the UK's OM in a political tangle

Nearly 20 years after arriving in England, Manchester City's UAE owners could exit amid cheating charges. That would pose a challenge for a city — and prime minister — closely tied to Abu Dhabi.

Defiance was the word that stood out as Manchester City returned home for the first time since they were found guilty of 114 financial charges by the Premier League in a case that's made headlines around the world.

City showed a measure of it on the pitch, even as they lost 4-2 to holders, Qatari-owned Paris Saint-Germain, in the Champions League group stages on Wednesday.

Among the home fanbase at the Etihad Stadium, DW found universal support for City's Abu Dhabi owners — headed by Sheikh Mansour, who is also UAE's vice president and deputy prime minister.

"I'm all in with the ownership now," said local fan Matt. "If we end up back in League Two [fourth tier], so be it. We've got 15 years of memories we wouldn't have otherwise and to me it's just an accounting line on a spreadsheet really."

The stadium name itself is a reminder of the takeover that changed this club, and its part of Manchester, in 2008. The national airline of the UAE, Etihad's inflated sponsorship of City is among the key planks of the case against them, while many buildings and businesses around the stadium bear the brand. It's a stadium that's also seen plenty of success of late.

"We've seen things other fans will never see," added fellow City fan Josh. "I think people have got to consider how many people this club has given great memories to, how many people's lives have been changed by it, myself included....you've got to be grateful for that."

Though many in sky blue shared those sentiments, most outside the club now view the trophies won in the relevant period as tarnished. On top of that, critics point to City's reticence in providing evidence in their case, the long-documented human rights abuses in UAE and the often dismissive tone with which the owners have dealt with being found guilty of charges they have now appealed.

But those City fans appear to have one powerful ally in British Prime Minister Andy Burnham. Though he has since sought to tone down his position on Mansour and co. somewhat, the former mayor of Greater Manchester said he "would be really concerned to lose them," after the verdict was announced in late September. "They've been such a huge partner in the building of modern Manchester," he added.

While Burnham has since denied being close to City's ownership, even as City were playing on Wednesday, The Guardian reported a club representative was invited on a government-backed trade delegation to India this week by Burnham's successor as Manchester mayor, Bev Craig.

Earlier the same day, Burnham was forced to confirm he had two additional dinners with the club chair, Khaldoon Al Mubarak, at the Etihad Stadium, having previously declared those only as matchday hospitality. It has become a serious domestic issue for the man who replaced Keir Starmer in July.

What is clear is that Burnham welcomed Abu Dhabi investment during his time in charge of the northwestern city. This came mainly in the form of residential and commercial buildings as well as libraries and sports centers, all popping up in previously run-down areas in and around the stadium.

Georgina Blakeley, a Professor at the University of Huddersfield who wrote a book called "The regeneration of east Manchester," told DW that UAE investment has transformed swathes of the city.

"One of the prime drivers for building this partnership with the Abu Dhabi United Group [a private equity arm of the state mostly concerned with the football club] was that at that point in time, 2014, there was no other private investor willing to invest at the scale that the council wanted. And to do so in a way that created a build-to-rent market," she said.

The UAE investment in Manchester, accelerated by the Manchester Life Partnership with the city's council signed in 2014, has been a key defense for the City owners and fans when faced with sportswashing allegations in the past. And while Blakeley believes councils in the UK are often forced to seek foreign investment, there has been some pushback on how east Manchester has changed.

"A lot of people would be critical of some of the developments in [the districts of] Ancoats and New Islington and say that it's gentrified the area and there are some tensions between newer populations coming in and living in the more expensive flats and those already there. I think there's some truth to it. But I also think there's some truth to the fact that people forget really, really quickly that if you go back to just the 1990s, Ancoats was a no-go area," she said.

While profit for both council and state have certainly been a driver of the partnership, Blakeley thinks that forging greater links to the community could be something City's owners see "as a beneficial side effect."

After 18 years in the club and city, the UAE are deeply embedded in this part of Manchester. With political ties between the UK and UAE now reportedly strained as a result of the charges, there are suggestions that City's owners will try to sell the club. They may also be forced to do so, with sanctions still to be decided pending City's appeal.

There is no such trouble at PSG, who look capable of winning a third straight Champions League powered by their own Gulf-State coffers.

What is clear is that state-owned football clubs can become successful fast in a free market, as the Premier League was when the UAE owners arrived. Deep pockets, strong contacts at the top tables of the sport and canny strategies to integrate with the local area can be a heady mix.

It's certainly one that's proved intoxicating to City's fans, who brush off secret second contracts, sham sponsorship and unreliable witnesses with a loyalty built on success, community and investment. Judging by the mood in Manchester on Wednesday, no sanctions are going to change that.

Real Madrid president Florentino Perez and manager Jose Mourinho have turned their attentions to the Premier League in the hunt for a marquee midfield signing, according to reports.

Los Blancos closed the gap on La Liga leaders Barcelona with a 2-1 El Clasico win on Sunday, but they have not won the title or Champions League since 2023/24. That's only a couple of years for any other club, but Real Madrid seldom suffer droughts without a throaty reaction in the transfer market.

Perez is a man of big promises and Liverpool could be the next club who can expect to have to resist Real Madrid's overtures for one of their most important players.

According to reports in Spain, Perez and Mourinho have 'once again' identified Reds midfielder Alexis Mac Allister as their top target for the summer of 2027. The Argentinian international's contract status is a factor in their temptation.

"Perez wants to take advantage of the fact that the player has yet to renew his contract to try and convince him to join in January 2027, although his contract expires in June 2028 and any early signing would require negotiating a transfer fee," reports Fichajes.

"His profile fits the need for a midfielder who provides balance without sacrificing creativity. Mac Allister can play in front of the defence, press opposing midfielders, and link up with attackers through through balls."

Mac Allister signed a five-year contract when he moved to Anfield and is now in the last two years of that deal, alerting potential suitors to the opportunity to test Liverpool's need to bring in a transfer fee if and when he leaves.

The looming interest from Spain could play a part in any contract negotiations that do take place, but Real Madrid can expect to spend big if they want to make their move before Mac Allister's deal runs out.

#775. Sonys new XM4C headphones are some of the best Ive tested this year — theyre down to a new record low

Save $50: The Sony WH-1000XM4C are $50 off for the first time ever — and at a new record low price — thanks to an on-page coupon at Amazon as of Oct. 26. This brings their price down to $248 from the list price of $299.99, saving you 16% off their total list price.

The XM4C are a return to form. Where Sony's newer flagships, the XM5 and XM6, opt for a more varied design, the XM4C stick to the extremely popular look of the XM4 — an unassuming, yet classic pair of headphones. Especially at this price point, these headphones are over $100 cheaper than Bose's mid-range option. I'd be surprised to see a much better deal over Black Friday, so if you're looking to get your headphones shopping done early, the XM4Cs are definitely worth your consideration.

#773. Coach banned until 2048 for training children in DR Congo while suspended for sexual abuse

Jonathan Bukabakwa gets extra five-year ban from Fifa

DR Congo’s FA must improve education and compliance

A coach in the Democratic Republic of the Congo who was banned from football for 20 years by Fifa for sexual abuse of a minor has had his ban extended by five years to 2048 by the world governing body.

Jonathan Bukabakwa, known as Messi, has also been ordered to pay an additional fine of $15,000 (£11,200) after the Guardian revealed he had continued to work with young players despite his suspension.

The Congolese Association Football Federation (Fecofa) and Racing Club Baki, where Bukabakwa was coaching, have been fined $50,000 each by Fifa’s ethics committee for failing to comply with its decision to ban him for 20 years in 2023.

In the Guardian app, tap the Profile settings button at the top right, then select Notifications. Turn on sport notifications.

If you already have the Guardian app, make sure you’re on the most recent version.

If you don't have the Guardian app, download it from the iOS App Store on iPhone or the Google Play store on Android by searching for 'The Guardian'.

Fecofa has also been instructed to introduce a “comprehensive education, compliance and monitoring programme for its officials, affiliated clubs and registered members” to try to ensure there is no repeat.

“The Congo DR Football Association, the club Racing Club Baki (formerly known as Esperanto FC), and the coach Jonathan Bukabakwa were found responsible for failing to comply with the decision rendered by the Fifa ethics committee on 18 October 2023,” said a Fifa statement.

Bukabakwa, who previously coached youth teams in the Limpopo and Malabo regions, was convicted of sexually abusing minors by authorities in the DRC and was released from prison last year. A Guardian investigation in August revealed there was video footage of him coaching young players at Esperanto in the Limete district of the capital, Kinshasa. Local sources confirmed he had regularly been seen taking charge of players on a school field.

Bukabakwa did not deny continuing to coach young players but claimed he had not been informed of his Fifa ban. He said he had not been questioned or received any communication from the governing body. He has denied the allegations against him.

The case has raised further questions about safeguarding in Congolese football and the ability of authorities to ensure that suspended individuals do not continue participating in the sport. Bukabakwa was provisionally suspended by Fecofa in November 2022 after media reports of alleged widespread sexual abuse in the DRC. Several other coaches were provisionally suspended at the time, although investigations into some of those other cases did not establish sufficient evidence for further action by Fecofa or Fifa.

Véron Mosengo-Omba, elected as Fecofa’s president in May, has promised to look into the previous cases once he has re-established a disciplinary committee. Fecofa was under a Fifa-appointed normalisation committee from 2023 until his election. “We will make sure that no one who is behaving like this will be involved in football in this country,” he said.

#771. A massive asteroid slammed into the North Sea and unleashed a 330-foot tsunami

A massive asteroid slammed into the North Sea roughly 43 to 46 million years ago, triggering a tsunami more than 100 meters (330 feet) high. Now, after more than two decades of scientific disagreement, researchers have uncovered compelling evidence confirming the ancient collision.

The impact created what is now known as the Silverpit Crater, a massive structure buried deep beneath the seafloor. Although scientists first discovered it in 2002, its origins remained fiercely debated, with some researchers arguing that geological processes, rather than an object from space, were responsible.

A research team led by Dr. Uisdean Nicholson of Heriot-Watt University in Edinburgh has now provided the strongest evidence yet that Silverpit was formed by an asteroid or comet. By combining advanced seismic imaging, microscopic examination of rock samples, and computer simulations, the scientists have reconstructed the violent event and identified minerals that could only have formed under extraordinary pressure.

The findings, published in Nature Communications, establish Silverpit as one of the relatively few confirmed impact craters on Earth.

A Mysterious Crater Beneath the North Sea

The Silverpit Crater lies approximately 700 meters beneath the seabed in the southern North Sea, about 80 miles off the coast of Yorkshire, England. The crater itself measures roughly three kilometers across and is surrounded by a much larger, 20-kilometer-wide region of circular faults, which are fractures in the rock where movement has occurred.

When scientists first identified the formation in 2002, several features suggested that it had been created by a high-speed collision with an asteroid or comet.

Its nearly circular outline, elevated central peak, and surrounding rings of faults resembled structures found at other impact sites. Central peaks can develop when rocks compressed by a powerful collision rebound upward, somewhat like the way water rises in the center after an object splashes into a pool.

Some geologists proposed that the unusual formation resulted from the movement of underground salt deposits, which can deform the rock layers above them. Others suggested that volcanic activity had caused part of the seabed to collapse, producing a structure that only appeared to be an impact crater.

The disagreement persisted for years. In 2009, geologists even voted on the competing explanations, as documented in Geoscientist magazine. Most participants rejected the asteroid impact hypothesis.

The latest findings show that the impact explanation was correct.

Scientists Find the Smoking Gun for an Ancient Asteroid Impact

The breakthrough came from combining newly available seismic images with microscopic evidence recovered from beneath the seabed.

Seismic imaging allows geologists to investigate structures hidden underground by analyzing how sound waves travel through different layers of rock. This technique can reveal buried faults, craters, and other formations that cannot be observed directly.

Using the improved data, Nicholson and his colleagues were able to examine Silverpit's internal structure in unprecedented detail.

But the most decisive evidence came from tiny mineral crystals discovered in rock cuttings collected from an oil well near the crater.

Dr. Uisdean Nicholson, a sedimentologist in Heriot-Watt University's School of Energy, Geoscience, Infrastructure and Society, said: "New seismic imaging has given us an unprecedented look at the crater.

"Samples from an oil well in the area also revealed rare 'shocked' quartz and feldspar crystals at the same depth as the crater floor.

"We were exceptionally lucky to find these -- a real 'needle-in-a-haystack' effort. These prove the impact crater hypothesis beyond doubt, because they have a fabric that can only be created by extreme shock pressures."

Shocked minerals are particularly important because their internal crystal structures have been altered by immense pressure. Such changes can occur when an asteroid strikes at tremendous speed, generating powerful shock waves that pass through the surrounding rock.

Ordinary geological processes generally cannot produce these distinctive microscopic features. Their discovery at the same depth as the crater floor provides strong physical evidence that an extremely violent impact occurred.

A 160-Meter Asteroid Unleashed a Monster Tsunami

With the crater's origins established, researchers used numerical models, or computer simulations based on physical laws, to reconstruct what happened during the collision.

Their results suggest that an asteroid approximately 160 meters across approached from the west and struck the seabed at a shallow angle.

Although much smaller than the asteroid associated with the extinction of the dinosaurs, an object of this size could still release an enormous amount of energy when traveling at cosmic speeds.

According to the simulations, the impact violently displaced vast quantities of rock and seawater, producing a towering curtain of debris before the material collapsed back into the ocean.

Dr. Nicholson said: "Our evidence shows that a 160-meter-wide asteroid hit the seabed at a low angle from the west.

"Within minutes, it created a 1.5-kilometer-high curtain of rock and water that then collapsed into the sea, creating a tsunami over 100 meters high."

That means the collision sent material nearly a mile into the air before it fell back into the sea, generating a tsunami taller than a 30-story building.

Unlike ordinary wind-driven waves, tsunamis involve the movement of enormous volumes of water. They are typically generated when a sudden disturbance, such as an earthquake, underwater landslide, or asteroid impact, rapidly displaces the water above it.

The estimated height of Silverpit's tsunami comes from computer modeling of the ancient impact rather than direct observation. Nevertheless, the reconstruction illustrates just how destructive even a relatively modest asteroid can be when it strikes the ocean.

A Scientific Debate Finally Settled After Decades

For Professor Gareth Collins of Imperial College London, the discovery brings a satisfying conclusion to a controversy he witnessed firsthand.

Collins participated in the 2009 debate over Silverpit's origins and contributed the numerical simulations used in the new research.

Professor Collins said: "I always thought that the impact hypothesis was the simplest explanation and most consistent with the observations.

"It is very rewarding to have finally found the silver bullet. We can now get on with the exciting job of using the amazing new data to learn more about how impacts shape planets below the surface, which is really hard to do on other planets. "

The findings do more than settle an old argument. They also give scientists a valuable opportunity to study the underground damage caused by high-speed asteroid impacts.

Because much of Silverpit's structure remains buried and well preserved, researchers can investigate how the collision fractured, displaced, and reshaped the surrounding geological layers.

These observations could help scientists interpret impact structures on other worlds, where the subsurface effects of collisions are often much more difficult to examine.

Earth has experienced countless asteroid and comet impacts throughout its history, but relatively few recognizable craters remain.

Weathering, erosion, sediment deposition, and plate tectonics continually reshape the planet's surface. Over millions of years, these processes can wear down, bury, or completely destroy evidence of ancient collisions.

As a result, exceptionally preserved impact sites like Silverpit are scientifically valuable.

Dr. Nicholson said, "Silverpit is a rare and exceptionally preserved hypervelocity impact crater.

"These are rare because the Earth is such a dynamic planet -- plate tectonics and erosion destroy almost all traces of most of these events.

"Around 200 confirmed impact craters exist on land, and only about 33 have been identified beneath the ocean.

"We can use these findings to understand how asteroid impacts shaped our planet throughout history, as well as predict what could happen should we have an asteroid collision in future."

Hypervelocity impacts occur when objects strike at extremely high speeds, typically several kilometers per second. At those velocities, even an asteroid measuring only a few hundred meters across can deliver a devastating amount of energy.

With its origins now confirmed, Silverpit joins a small group of important underwater and buried impact structures, including the Chicxulub Crater in Mexico, associated with the mass extinction that wiped out the nonavian dinosaurs, and the Nadir Crater off the coast of West Africa, another recently confirmed asteroid impact site.

Together, these craters provide evidence of the powerful role cosmic collisions have played in shaping Earth's geological and biological history.

By studying Silverpit's remarkably preserved features, scientists hope to better understand what happens when an asteroid strikes the ocean, how the surrounding rock responds, and what such an event might mean for Earth in the future.

The research was funded by the Natural Environment Research Council (NERC).

Materials provided by Heriot-Watt University. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#770. Never finishing the fight: Where did Xbox go wrong with Halo?
