After a failed soft reboot and a remake of the original, the Halo universe is facing yet another major creative shift. But has Xbox learned any lessons this time?

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Halo: Campaign Evolved was Xbox’s second attempt at refreshing the 2001 sci-fi FPS classic, and it is perfectly fine. A cromulent remake, if ever there was one, but it’s hardly a franchise-saver, especially as Xbox looks to rethink its studio structure and approach to renowned IPs like Halo or Gears of War.

Halo hasn’t been the same since original developer Bungie — now owned by Sony and working on Destiny and Marathon — departed, leaving 343 Industries (now Halo Studios) to guide the franchise.

Neither Halo 4 nor 5 meaningfully evolved the gameplay formula or overarching narrative. Halo Infinite found more success by reheating what “old Halo felt like” and sticking it into a half-baked open world, but it still felt far from the series’ glory days.

This year’s full remake of Combat Evolved's campaign delivered exactly what it promised: a shiny, 2026 version of the game that made audiences fall in love with the franchise. It was a clear attempt to reignite faith in 343’s vision, but how is a faithful, shinier remake proof of anything?

It might not even matter anymore, as the new Xbox leadership has rocked the ship again, leaving Master Chief and the universe’s future cloudier than ever before. Now, with rumors of a reboot being considered at new steward Activision, is there hope for the Spartans, or will Xbox run into another dead end?

It looks like the ‘Halo Studios’ moniker only lasted a single (remade) game, as Xbox has recently announced that Call of Duty maker and publisher Activision — which houses many FPS-oriented studios — is taking over development, essentially leaving the 343 veterans as a minor support studio, which translates into more layoffs.

If we put aside the human and institutional cost of this whole back-and-forth over the years (a big ask in today’s climate), there are reasons to be optimistic about the quality of an Activision-developed Halo. Regardless of what you think about the Call of Duty IP in 2026 and the publisher’s overall vision for it, it’s clear there’s plenty of talent holding everything together and, more importantly, enough experience to iterate on what Halo’s multiplayer side needs to shine bright again.

But this won’t fix the larger issue with Halo, which is that Master Chief should’ve been left behind long ago. Like it or not, it’s difficult to deny Halo’s “golden era” ended with Halo: Reach (Bungie’s swansong under Xbox) and the Halo Wars spinoffs, which took the series into the real-time strategy genre. These games were wildly different, but they had one core thing in common: they made the sci-fi setting feel bigger instead of smaller, offering the perfect blueprint to build upon in a post-Halo 3 world.

There’s nothing inherently wrong with the Master Chief. Franchises can be built around iconic characters like Mario or Lara Croft. But in the post-Bungie era, his role changed from an extremely competent Spartan soldier to a space messiah. Halo 4, 5, and Infinite used sci-fi nonsense to transform him into this larger-than-life figure, rendering the rest of the UNSC largely powerless in the process.

It’s the same mistake ID Software made with Doomguy, centering the story on how super special he is, and not on the world he inhabits. Now rumours are rumbling that Activision’s reboot might focus on Chief’s origins; that's the last thing Halo needs.

Forgetting Activision’s next Halo for a second, we were teased with (even if there wasn’t an official confirmation from Xbox) at least a Halo 2 remake via Halo: Campaign Evolved’s alternative ending.

Back in May 2026, renowned insider Rebs Gaming confidently reported that Halo 2 and 3 remakes were in development. But can fans realistically expect them after new Xbox CEO Asha Sharma’s latest shakeup?

Halo Studios could have done a "fine" job with safe remakes of the two original Halo sequels while Activision handles new projects. It’s hard to reject that approach, especially after Xbox finding success with Bethesda’s The Elder Scrolls IV: Oblivion Remastered and confirming two Fallout remasters are in the works. It seems like money waiting to be made, but the remakes could be cooked too.

Xbox and 343/Halo Studios have made plenty of mistakes with Halo, but the killer has always been their lack of conviction in their ideas. And look, nostalgia shouldn’t be the driving force behind a franchise in dire need of reinvention, but I’m far more annoyed by the creative rollbacks, flip-flopping, and now the loss of knowledge and talent accompanying the gutting of Halo Studios.

We haven’t even touched on the mess that was the live-action Halo show. It lasted only two seasons, had John-117 walking around outside the Mjolnir armor for most of its runtime, and was cancelled just as the story finally got to the titular Halo. Why would you stray so far from the source material, and how do you miss the mark so badly?

In many ways, it perfectly encapsulated what’s been wrong with Halo as a whole for so many years. The powers that be can’t decide what they really want from it, and thus it’s stuck, unable to leave the past behind or convincingly establish a new horizon for itself. Other franchises have run into the same issues — looking at you, Star Wars — but Halo has been stuck in this purgatory for 15+ years, as Xbox leaders and strategy shifts come and go.

Could Xbox’s newly-formed XP division deliver the focused big-screen adaptation the series has always deserved? I’m sure that’s the plan, but even a cursory glance back at Xbox’s handling of the franchise for the past decade or so says no.

Halo’s deep problems won’t be fixed overnight, and throwing a bunch of hard-working developers to the curb certainly won’t help. Halo’s current struggles didn’t come from Halo Studios or 343 Industries; they came from impatient and incompetent leadership calling the property-defining shots. I’ve seen nothing to suggest that’s changed.

Fran Ruiz is our resident Star Wars guy. His hunger for movies and TV series is only matched by his love for video games. He got a BA of English Studies, focusing on English Literature, from the University of Malaga, in Spain, as well as a Master's Degree in English Studies, Multilingual and Intercultural Communication. On top of writing features and other longform articles for Space.com since 2021, he is a frequent collaborator of VG247 and other gaming sites. He also serves as associate editor over at Star Wars News Net and its sister site, Movie News Net.

Kane was destined for the top from an early age, according to an ex-Tottenham Hotspur teammate

2026 Ballon d'Or winner Harry Kane made light work of his competition as a young player at Tottenham Hotspur, a former teammate at the North London club has admitted.

Spurs legend Kane scored 213 goals for the club in the Premier League, leaving in 2023 as the league's second-highest goalscorer. His move to Bayern Munich laid the foundations for back-to-back Bundesliga titles, to 61 goals in 51 matches in 2025/26 and, ultimately, to the Ballon d'Or.

#768. Apple breaks silence on iPhone Duo cracking reports

After days of viral reports about the iPhone Duo's inner screen, Apple has publicly acknowledged the problem.

In a statement provided to Mashable, the company said it is aware of an issue affecting the inner display on a "small number" of units.

"The response to iPhone Duo from early users has been incredibly positive, and we are seeing exciting things from developers who are rapidly updating their apps to take advantage of its unique form factor,” Apple said. “We are aware of an issue impacting the inner display on a small number of iPhone Duo units. If users experience issues with their iPhone Duo display, they should contact AppleCare for a replacement."

The statement doesn’t say what’s causing the problem, how many units are affected, or whether Apple plans a broader fix. Apple appears to have sent the same statement to several other outlets covering the reports. Still, it’s the company’s first public acknowledgment of the problem.

The Duo has drawn plenty of attention as Apple’s first foldable iPhone. It sold out almost immediately when pre-orders opened Oct. 16, with some delivery estimates stretching into January 2027. But early owners have found that it’s very much a first-generation device, and the display reports are just one of several complaints.

Beyond durability, users have flagged a few other issues, as reported by Mashable Senior Editor Stan Schroeder. First, as JerryRigsEverything points out, the protective matte screen on the foldable dulls its color and vibrance, and it picks up heavy glare. The FaceTime camera, as Schroeder notes, is bad for photos, with Apple leaning heavily on AI processing that can leave selfies looking plasticky and smudged. Finally, many apps aren’t yet optimized for the Duo’s inner display, including Apple’s own App Store — though Schroeder reported that he expects app developers to catch up within the next month.

Durability concerns had been building before the display reports went viral. YouTuber Tim Pan ran the Duo through 10,000 folds in a folding machine, plus another 1,000 at -4 degrees Fahrenheit. Before-and-after images from the test spread online, appearing to show a much deeper crease.

Samsung seems to be enjoying this. While the Korean tech giant hasn’t taken any direct shots at the Duo, at least not officially, its Samsung Mobile US account has been playing reply guy under iPhone Duo posts on X. One reply simply read "mogged. respectfully."

Cringe looks-maxxing speak aside, Samsung is getting plenty of free marketing out of the Duo’s rocky start. The company is now on its eighth-generation Galaxy Z Fold. Although its foldables went through their own early durability problems, Samsung has had years to refine the hinge, crease, and display that Apple is only attempting for the first time. Every viral post about a cracked Duo screen is, in effect, an ad for the competition.

If your Duo’s inner display is showing problems, Apple recommends contacting AppleCare for a replacement.

#766. Why Chinese tech brands are outpacing Western rivals from EVs to robot vacuums

From action cameras and smartphones to EVs and robot vacuums, Chinese brands are gaining ground worldwide, despite privacy and security concerns. What's driving their rise, and can European rivals keep up?

Do you remember the days when GoPro was synonymous with action cameras?

At its peak in 2015, the US company reigned supreme with around 6.6 million units sold that year and a market share of over 70%. Today, the pioneer is fighting for survival.

GoPro's biggest rivals, DJI and Insta360, entered the action camera market in 2019 and 2021, respectively. Today, they account for more than 90% of global smart handheld camera sales.

Alexander Hannemann, who regularly uses action cameras to film his motorcycle rides, says he switched from GoPro to Insta360 around two years ago.

"The quality is much better than a GoPro, even when I'm speeding up and going into corners at like 80 kilometers per hour," he told DW.

GoPro's case is not an exception. Since around 2010, many Chinese brands have not only caught up but surpassed once-dominant Western rivals across the consumer technology industry, from smart TVs to robot vacuums.

Gone are the days when "Made in China" stood for cheap, mass-produced products.

"China is still making affordable products today," says Chris Pereira, a Canadian entrepreneur and author with over 20 years of experience in China. "But the products are also high quality."

This combination has led to well over half of new consumer tech products globally coming from Chinese suppliers, Pereira adds.

BYD, Xpeng and other Chinese car manufactures sold almost two-thirds of all electric cars last year, surpassing incumbents like Tesla. In robot vacuums, Chinese brands dominate the global market, leaving former pioneer US company iRobot struggling to compete.

So why do Chinese companies increasingly outperform their Western rivals?

Pereira, whose consultancy iMpact helps Chinese companies gain traction overseas, attributes China's success to fierce domestic competition in cities like Shenzhen, Hangzhou and Shanghai; highly integrated supply chains and a can-do business culture that allows companies to innovate; and respond to problems quickly.

Other key reasons for China's competitive edge include unparalleled industrial automation, the strategic use of AI in manufacturing, responsiveness from regulators as well as relatively cheap labor, which together leads to significantly lower production costs and much faster innovation cycles than in the world's most advanced economies.

Joey Cheng, who moved to the US for his PhD studies over 20 years ago, is familiar with both the American consumer and the Chinese producer sides.

While his startup is based in the US, almost all the manufacturing is done in Shenzhen, where a large share of the world's hardware and consumer electronics are made.

"The supply chain in Shenzhen is basically inevitable for cost cutting and quick product development," Cheng says. "Especially as a startup you must get the product out quickly, or you'll get blown out of the water."

Although consumers are flocking to Chinese companies, some reservations persist.

A recent poll found that more than 90% of Europeans do not trust Chinese technology companies with their data, while about 80% distrust US firms. Germans expressed the highest level of mistrust.

Nadine, a German travel blogger who uses DJI and Insta360 products, says she wouldn't use any Asian products at home.

"My home robot vacuum is from a German company for precisely this reason: So the data remains compliant with German regulations. After all, manufacturers can connect directly to the camera," she said, referring to camera and audio hacking incidents involving robot vacuums.

Still, Pereira says that Chinese companies expanding abroad have to pay a "trust premium."

"This trust deficit means that they need to be extra competitive in terms of product and price in order for the market to choose them," he says, adding that the gap can be narrowed by a presence in overseas markets like BYD's first European passenger-car plant, expected to open in Hungary later this year.

Chinese companies say they comply with EU data protection rules. However, domestic security laws in the US and China can require firms to hand over data to authorities, and German officials note limited oversight of whether data is transferred from European servers abroad.

In addition to privacy concerns, Chinese consumer tech firms have come under scrutiny over espionage and other risks, most recently regarding connected devices like EVs.

In light of security concerns, trade deficits and China's overall dominance, the US and several other Western countries have hit Chinese imports and companies with tariffs, blacklists and strict requirements. In late July, the Trump administration banned the import of humanoid robots manufactured outside the US.

A representative of Chinese robot maker Primebot told DW that all Chinese consumer tech companies are feeling the effects.

"We might seek cooperation with a local US company. Another potential solution is building a factory there," he said.

European countries and the EU are also weighing more tariffs against Chinese companies.

Chris Pereira foresees that the tariffs will accelerate two trends among Chinese companies: Moving more manufacturing overseas and an increasing focus on challenging top-tier Western brands with premium goods.

"Chinese officials and companies recognize that manufacturing needs to be localized to a certain degree. One reason is to assuage the challenges of exporting, another is to create trust locally," Pereira sums up.

"The other trend is that once duties wipe out the price advantage at the bottom, the margin left is at the top, so Chinese brands are increasingly competing directly with established Western names, where trust matters most."

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

As China's dominance keeps growing, Western companies are scrambling to find ways to stay competitive.

Kaz Merchant, who works for a UK-based company that imports and exports IT hardware, says smaller companies, in particular, must carve out a geographic niche.

"They need to have their own unique selling point for a certain product, which China might not have for that region. Or they need to partner with a Chinese company," he says.

To Pereira, a key issue Europe needs to solve is regulators' responsiveness.

"Chinese regulators tell you very clearly and quickly what you can do. That speed helps companies iterate and succeed overseas," Pereira says.

"If you're waiting weeks for a response, you're going to lose business."

#763. Honey bees are bullying other bees, scientists discover

Honey bees are proving to be formidable competitors for food in the UK, and scientists have discovered that they frequently use aggression to drive other pollinators away from flowers. New research has documented how honey bees chase, displace, and intimidate rival insects, providing the first detailed evidence of how extensively they interfere with other species' access to food.

The study, conducted by researchers at Anglia Ruskin University (ARU) and published in Ecological Entomology, followed pollinator activity over two years at five locations in and around Cambridge, England. Each site was situated within 300 meters of an established honey bee colony.

Researchers observed insects visiting bramble (Rubus fruticosus) and ivy (Hedera helix), two flowering plants that provide important supplies of nectar and pollen. Both attract large numbers of insects during their flowering periods, making them useful places to investigate competition for food.

Honey Bees Dominate the Competition for Flowers

To examine how different pollinators interact, the team divided flower visitors into five groups: honey bees, bumble bees, solitary bees, hoverflies, and an 'other' category containing butterflies, beetles, and wasps.

Over 93 hours of observation, the scientists documented encounters in which one insect interrupted another's feeding or prevented it from reaching a flower. Each interaction was classified according to its outcome. A 'win' occurred when one insect gained access to a flower at the expense of another, which experienced a 'loss' of feeding opportunities. A 'share' meant both insects were able to access the flower without conflict.

The results revealed a clear pattern. Locations with greater concentrations of honey bees generally had fewer other pollinators and experienced more competition for flowers. Honey bees also emerged victorious in a larger proportion of their encounters with other insects.

On bramble flowers, doubling the density of honey bees was associated with a 14% decrease in bumblebee abundance. On ivy, higher honey bee numbers were linked to reduced numbers of solitary bees and hoverflies.

The honey bees did not simply reach flowers before their competitors. They frequently initiated aggressive encounters, driving other insects away to secure feeding opportunities.

Other pollinators actively avoided honey bees in 28% of observed interactions on bramble and 36% of those on ivy. In at least 20% of the encounters honey bees won on either plant, they physically pushed aside or chased away competing insects.

Aggressive behavior initiated by species other than honey bees was uncommon. However, one insect stood out as an exception.

The ivy bee, a solitary species not native to Britain that was first documented in the country in 2001, was the only pollinator observed actively challenging honey bees. Unlike most other insects, ivy bees sometimes defended their feeding spots by chasing or displacing honey bees, successfully competing for access to flowers.

Climate Change Could Make Competition Worse

The findings are part of a broader research effort to understand competition among pollinators and identify ways to reduce its ecological effects.

The results also come as beekeeping continues to expand across the UK. The number of registered beekeepers increased from 19,000 in 2010 to 47,000 last year, reflecting growing numbers of managed honey bee colonies.

Researchers warn that rising temperatures could add to the pressure on wild pollinators. Their observations showed that competitive encounters became more frequent during warmer conditions.

One possible explanation involves changes in flower nectar production. When plants experience 'heat stress', they can produce less nectar, potentially forcing more insects to compete over a smaller supply of food.

Temperature also affects different pollinators in different ways. Bumblebees and hoverflies are sensitive to high temperatures and may forage more effectively in cooler weather. Honey bees, by contrast, generally become more active as temperatures rise. Previous research found that increasing the temperature by 12°C caused a tenfold increase in the number of honey bees leaving their nests.

Together, these differences suggest that continued warming could give honey bees an even greater competitive advantage over some wild pollinators.

Protecting Wild Pollinators as Honey Bee Numbers Grow

Lead author Victoria Buckle, a PhD student at Anglia Ruskin University (ARU) in Cambridge, England, emphasized that the research is intended to improve conservation efforts rather than discourage people from keeping honey bees.

"The aim of our study was not to discourage beekeeping, but to better understand how competition happens between wild and managed pollinator species with the aim of helping to conserve our wild pollinators, which play such an important ecological role.

"We found that most interactions when competing for food were instigated by honey bees and they had a greater number of 'wins', resulting in a loss of forage for other pollinators. Bumblebees, solitary bees and hoverflies would avoid, be displaced by and on some occasions be chased off by honey bees.

"An interesting exception was the ivy bee, which would win contests more often against honey bees. It is the first time, to our knowledge, such behaviors have been observed and shows ivy bees are able to 'hold their own' in such encounters.

"However, honey bees outcompeted all other wild pollinators and based on our findings, future predicted warming in the UK is only likely to further exacerbate interference competition from dominant honey bees. Therefore, it's important to find ways to ensure honey bees and wild pollinators can continue to coexist, particularly in locations with a high density of hives, such as urban areas."

Materials provided by Anglia Ruskin University. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

This incredible composite image of Orionid meteors was captured as Earth passed through the debris trail shed by Halley's Comet.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Astrophotographer Tim McCollum captured a spectacular view of Orionid meteors sharing the night sky with a host of deep sky wonders on Oct. 21, as the annual shooting star display ramped up to peak activity.

Comet 1P/Halley may be over 3 billion miles (4.8 kilometers) from Earth, but that didn't stop it from putting on a breathtaking display earlier this week, as particles cast off by the ancient wanderer carved a fiery path through the autumn sky.

McCollum was able to capture a trio of vivid Orionid meteors as they streaked through the night sky above the city of Toccoa, Georgia, between 3-4 a.m. on Oct. 21. Each of the shooting stars can be seen racing away from the shower's apparent point of origin — or radiant — which is located close to the red giant star Betelgeuse in the constellation Orion.

Successfully imaging a shooting star takes a combination of skill, luck and patience. McCollum photographed the meteors over the course of multiple 30-second exposures taken with a Canon EOS Ra camera and RF 28-70 f2.0 lens, which was fixed unerringly on the radiant with the aid of a star tracker. The individual exposures were then combined into a single composite image using the astrophoto software PixInsight.

The Canon EOS R7 is the best camera for beginners. Despite its crop sensor, the EOS R7 can rival most full-frame cameras. It features a 34.4MP sensor, ISO range up to 32,000 and a video capability of 4K UHD 60p. It has the best tools to help a beginner master photography. Read our full Canon EOS R7 review.

The familiar stars of Orion's Belt glisten to the right of McCollum's image, close to the glow of the Orion Nebula — one of the nearest major star forming regions to Earth. The Rosette Nebula (Caldwell 49) can also be spotted embedded in the dense starfield and dust lanes of the Milky Way's galactic plane, which runs diagonally through the composite image, as can numerous star clusters.

The Orionid meteor shower is active until Nov. 7, so be sure to head outside over the next few nights for a chance to spot one of Comet 1P/Halley's dazzling shooting stars. If you're lucky, you may even spot a Halloween Fireball belonging to the Taurid meteor shower, as Earth passes through the debris streams cast off by Comet 2P/Encke!

Why not read our handy guide to photographing shooting stars? You can also take a look at our picks of the best lenses and cameras for astrophotography available in 2026, including the Canon EOS R8, which delivers the essential interval shooting functionality you need to capture meteors without bursting the bank.

Editor's Note: If you would like to share your astrophotography with Space.com's readers, then please send your photo(s), comments, and your name and location to spacephotos@space.com.

#760. Mentoring a lab colleague? Share your ‘CV of failures’ with them

Dom Byrne is a freelance radio and podcast producer based in Brighton, UK.

Melanie Stefan says early career researchers can learn from others’ failures.Your browser does not support the audio element.Download MP3See transcript

Melanie Stefan leads a computational neurobiology lab at MSB Medical School Berlin. But as a postdoc 16 years ago, a failed fellowship application prompted her to write an article in Nature. In it, she called for researchers to keep a ‘CV of failures.’

Stefan says: “The idea would be that the successful people in science would actually open up about the failures that they had on their way, and how many jobs they didn’t get, how many positions they weren’t admitted to, how many grants they failed to receive, how many papers were rejected, things like that, so that younger scientists (like I was at the time) could actually take heart and see that this is actually a normal part of the process and that they’re not alone.

“So that was the idea. And since then, I’ve kind of become the poster child of failure a little bit.”

This is the second episode of a six-part podcast series about failure in science, pegged to a conference held in April at Imperial College London, in partnership with Nature.

doi: https://doi.org/10.1038/d41586-026-03410-7

So I’m a chemist and experimental scientist, so I don’t need to search very hard for examples of day-to-day failure.

That’s Dr Alena Vdovchenko, a postdoctoral scientist at Imperial College London, talking in April this year at the Failure in Science conference.

In the lab, simply most of the things don’t work the way you plan them.

So sometimes you carefully design the experiment. You run it for sometimes weeks, months. It depends.

But then when you analyze the results, you realize that okay, you didn’t account for factor A.

You need to redesign the experiment, maybe read more literature, go back to the lab.

Alena was speaking at the session called A Taxonomy of Failure, where we heard early career researchers explaining how, in real life, (let’s put it like this), things often don’t go quite as planned.

But overall, I think a lot of chemistry is trial and error. And during my PhD, we have that quote printed in our lab.

Theory is when you know everything but nothing works.

Practice is when everything works but no one knows why.

In our lab, theory and practice are combined.

I feel like it’s very funny, but also very uncomfortably true.

I’m Dr Stephen Webster, director of the Good Science Project at Imperial College London, a programme that examines what we like about research culture, and what we don’t.

And in episode two, we hear panelist Melanie Stefan, professor of physiology at Berlin Medical School, talk about failure and the lab.

My first big experience of failure in science was when I was doing my masters in a developmental biology lab, and I really had a difficult time there.

I didn’t quite know how to troubleshoot experiments if they failed. I didn’t quite understand what I was doing. I think it was not the right place for me.

And the worst day was when my supervisor called me into his office and said, "Melanie, I think you should really look for jobs outside of science."

And that was devastating because I had been studying science for many years at the time. I saw myself as a scientist. I didn’t really have a Plan B.

So much of my identity was being a scientist and doing science.

Not just that I loved doing it, and I was really interested in it, but also I didn’t know what else I could do.

So this was really a devastating failure for me.

And I wrote an article about this in Nature. It’s also the only thing I have published in Nature.

Other journals exist, and since then I’ve kind of become the poster child of failure, kind of without intending it.

So at the Failure in Science conference at Imperial College, I talked about this article I wrote called A CV of Failures.

And the story of that is after my master’s degree, (which I ended up completing in the end), I did a PhD, and I had a very great time there.

I had a lot of fun. It was quite a successful time for me.

But then I was done with that, and I needed to apply for funding to go to the next stage of my career as a postdoc.

And it was really hard. It was really hard to find funding. And I was rejected from, a lot of my funding applications were rejected. I started doubting myself.

And I also thought that, "Oh my God, I’m the first person this has happenened to."

Because all the other scientists around me, obviously, or like the scientists who were like a bit further ahead they were further ahead because they were ultimately successful in securing a postdoc position.

And so all I saw around me was ultimately the successes.

So I felt like I was the only one who had ever failed. So that made it even worse, and so this gave me the idea of writing this article calling for a CV of failures.

So the idea would be that the successful people in science would actually open up about their failures that they had on their way, and how many jobs they didn’t get, how many positions they weren’t admitted to, how many grants they failed to receive, how many papers were rejected, things like that.

So that younger scientists like I was at the time could actually take heart and see that this is actually a normal part of the process and that they’re not alone.

So that was the idea. And since then, I’ve kind of become the poster child of failure a little bit.

A lot of friends tell me, "Oh, Melanie, I read something about failure. I was thinking of you."

I was like, "Thank you. That is so nice."

I think it’s generally really cool that there has been following up on this article a bigger discussion about failure in science that the conference was also part of.

I think that’s really lovely. And it’s really lovely to see people come together and talk about this concept.

The conference is called Failure in Science, but I’d like to smuggle in engineering. I know Mary already mentioned engineering in the last session, and I’m an engineer, and I think engineering has a very well developed tradition of understanding and learning from failure.

So I liked the the session that was called "A View from the Lab.

And it was especially nice to to hear from the more junior scientists about how they interacted with failure and how they experienced it.

But I also thought the moderator George Constantinides is one thing that really that I found really interesting about what he said was how he compared it to engineering.

And I think if I were to summarize the very broad literature on failure in engineering, at least my understanding is: number one, there is no failure without uncertainty.

And number two, structured ways from learning from failure are essential.

And I think those are things that we can carry forward into many different aspects of our work.

In general, I would kind of summarize by saying, in my view, if we want people to fly high, then we have to make it easy and safe to fail well.

And in engineering, you have to fail. Like, that’s how. You build something, you try a prototype and then it fails, and you learn from that.

You watch how exactly it failed, you analyze it, and then you build the next better version, and the next version, the next version.

And I think the point he was making, and I really agree with that, is that we should treat scientific careers in the same way, and that we need to give people spaces where it’s safe to try something and fail, and then to talk about it, and learn from it, and then do better the next time and try again.

And maybe fail in a different way, but then build something in the end that’s better. So I really like that engineering analogy.

So one thing that also came up in a lot of the discussions at the conference, and that I think is really important, is the question of who actually gets to talk about failure and who gets to fail.

So the reason why I’m invited to conferences like that is that ultimately I did finish my master’s degree, and ultimately I did become a scientist.

And this is why I get to talk about failure is in my scientific career is because ultimately I have succeeded.

And what about the people, though, who have failed?

But people who have set out to have a life in science and are now doing something else. We never hear from them, right, because they are not in that space anymore?

So I think it takes a certain amount of privilege and position to be allowed to talk about failure.

And also, when I was a young scientist and I was looking for positions, I was sometimes, like, googling my name, and then the first thing that came up was A CV of Failures.

And I thought that was really strategically not that clever of me.

But I was not in a position then where it was much less safe to open up about failures.

And now I’m in a position where I can do it because I have a professorship. Nothing is nothing bad is going to happen to me.

So this privilege of opening up about failure is really important.

And then also failure itself is something that you need quite a bit of privilege to do.

So for me, I was able to apply for several postdoc grants and failed to get them until there was finally one that I succeeded in getting.

I was in a position to do that because my institute was happy to pay me for another year after I finished my PhD, and my supervisor was happy to keep me around in the lab, and my visa status didn’t depend on me securing funding right away.

So, and I didn’t have family to take care of at that time.

So it’s really like a lot of privileges came together that allowed me to even fail that much.

And that’s also something we need to think about. Who gets to fail? And then who gets to talk about failure.

I always had this kind of secret plan B, which was to open a cinema.

And I think a lot of my friends in science, if you ask them, they always have like a secret plan B. And it’s usually to do with doing something lovely like opening a bakery or opening a bike shop.

And for me it was opening a cinema, a small indie cinema.

I don’t know if I would have done it actually if I left science. But it was kind of a nice fantasy to escape to, and it sometimes still is.

But I still think, "Oh, I can open a cinema if if everything else fails."

But I would not think of it as a failure.

Because I think there’s a lot of ways to leave science, or to leave academia, and do really interesting and really cool things.

So a lot of my friends who were at university with me are not practicing scientists anymore. Or at least not research scientists, but they do really cool things in industry, in education, in all kinds of sectors.

So this view of leaving science as a failure, I think, is quite problematic as well.

I think it’s really important to share failures as well, in order to distinguish what is a personal failure and what is a system failing you.

And if I submit a grant application and I don’t get the grant, that’s fine.

That might be that my idea wasn’t good enough. Or the grant wasn’t well enough thought out.

But if 100 of my friends submit grant applications and then only 10 of them, only five of them, get a grant, then that maybe points to there being not enough money for research or not enough funding.

And I think we need to open up about failures as well to see where there are places in the system where the system actually could be improved.

#757. Azeez to Andrés: seven smart-looking Premier League deadline day deals

An 18-year-old who has played at two major international tournaments and the division’s top clearance maker from last season also feature

A tricky, exciting, quick and versatile midfielder, Azeez has proven himself in the Championship with Millwall over the past two seasons and now deserves a chance to test himself in the top flight.

Brighton had been chasing him for a while and struck a deal on deadline day for a very reasonable reported initial £12.5m, rising to £15m. If he can make the step up and produce anything like his second-tier form, he’ll be a steal.

He is a high-volume producer at the sharp end of the pitch, constantly making things happen that threaten the opposition’s goal, but standing out most for his crossing. He ranked top of the Championship last season for open-play crosses (227) and expected assists from crosses (6.71 xA).

He also ranked fourth for dribble attempts (131), ninth for shots (95), sixth for chances created from open play (58). He created good-quality chances, too; only two players created more big chances than he did (18), and he ranked fourth for expected assists. His 9.12 xA alongside only seven assists suggests he may benefit from playing alongside better finishers.

A hugely exciting prospect. Despite being 18, Mbaye has significant and noteworthy experience under his belt. Last year he made six appearances, including in the final, for Senegal at the Africa Cup of Nations and this summer he made four appearances at the World Cup. He has also played in two Ligue 1 title-winning campaigns, including 2024-25, when he became the youngest player in PSG’s history to start a game, and won the Champions League in both of those seasons

He is a dribbling and ball-carrying menace, who always looks as if he’s about to do something special. Among players to play at least 900 minutes in Ligue 1 last season, he was 11th for carries (moving at least five metres with the ball) per 90, with 20.0, and 12th for progressive carries (moving at least five metres upfield with the ball) per 90, with 10.3. Most of the players above him in both lists are defenders, who naturally have more space to move into.

He was third for take-on-ending carries, or running with the ball for at least five metres before attempting to beat an opponent with a dribble, with 2.9 per 90. And he also has end product to boot. He scored at Afcon and the World Cup despite limited minutes and his 0.45 goals or assists per 90 for PSG last season is not to be sniffed at.

Danso had a pretty impossible task over his two years at Tottenham. He was the back-up to the first-choice centre-backs Cristian Romero and Micky van de Ven, playing intermittently on both sides of central defence in dysfunctional teams jumping between styles of play, and he very rarely played for any consistent run of games.

He was unfairly compared with the players he was replacing for much of his time there, but the truth is he was always reliable enough. He excelled, however, when Romero got injured during the run-in last season and he was called upon in Spurs’ time of need as they scraped clear of relegation.

He may not be the best on the ball – though he is hardly terrible – but he is an excellent deep defender. Among those to play at least 1,000 minutes in the Premier League last season, Danso ranked first for clearances (8.2 per 90) and headed clearances (6.3), and was fifth for aerial duels won (4.8 per 90).

He could thrive at Sunderland, who spent more of their out-of-possession game time in a low block than any other team in the Premier League last season (25%). Danso could be perfectly at home in this setup, paired alongside the player he beat into second place for clearances and headed clearances: Dan Ballard.

It was always said during Pep Guardiola’s time at Manchester City that attackers had their wings clipped in terms of individual freedom, limited to playing within the parameters of the manager’s meticulous gameplan.

But dribbling ability became increasingly important in the final years of his reign, with Jérémy Doku and Antoine Semenyo on the wings and Rayan Cherki in between. Doku (162) and Semenyo (144) ranked first and second for dribbles attempted in the Premier League last season, with Cherki ninth (111). They also ranked first (84), fourth (59) and eighth (51), respectively, for dribbles completed.

With the deadline-day addition of Ndiaye, City have gone in even harder on dribbling ability under Enzo Maresca. The Senegal international ranked second for dribbles attempted (136) and dribbles completed (73) in the Premier League last season.

Fernandez-Pardo’s versatility is a major attraction. He’s comfortable playing off the left and cutting in on to his right foot, or as a No 9. Although not the bulkiest, he has explosive pace and promises to be a threat in behind.

The Belgium international is arguably at his most threatening when running with the ball because he does so with such purpose. His 122 carries progressing the ball at least 10 metres upfield in Ligue 1 last term were bettered by only seven players, several of whom were defenders.

On top of that, he ranked top for carries into the penalty area (69) and seventh for carries ending with a shot or chance created (39).

Fofana appeared to be enjoying a swift rise in 2024-25, only for a serious ankle injury to massively disrupt his development in 2025-26. Sunderland will be hoping they can get him back to the levels shown the season before last, when he was a real livewire on Lyon’s left flank.

He contributed nine goal involvements (five goals, four assists) in 1,586 minutes in Ligue 1 in 2024-25, averaging one every 176 minutes. Only eight forwards (900+ mins) averaged more expected assists per 90 (0.25).

The most eye-catching aspects of his game, however, were his dynamism and pace. He could be difficult to stop once moving, and this made him a very useful outlet. Among Ligue 1 forwards (900+ mins), his 16.6 carries per 90 ranked him sixth, and he moved up to third for progressive carries per 90 (11.5).

Fofana is direct, exciting and has significant potential. He is a player Sunderland could make a profit on if he reaches the levels previously expected.

Andrés arrived to bolster Brighton’s midfield options after Carlos Baleba’s exit to Manchester United. Although a different type of midfielder to the Cameroonian, Chema is another with vast potential.

A product of Real Madrid’s academy, Chema joined Stuttgart last summer. During his sole campaign in the Bundesliga, he was in and out of the team but caught the eye at the base of their midfield.

Comparisons have been made to Rodri because of his physical attributes and nominal role as a holding midfielder. He doesn’t have anything like the same influence in possession but Chema is tidy on the ball.

It’s what he offers defensively that is most notable, however. He’s been broadly praised for his anticipation and reading of the game, ranking second among midfielders (900+ mins) since the start of last season for interceptions per 90 in the Bundesliga (2.3).

#756. Brazil's elections matter for the planet. This is why

Brazil is heading back to the polls, but the impact could be felt worldwide. With the Amazon vital to the health of the planet, the election could help shape its future.

Few national elections carry as much environmental weight as Brazil's presidential race. And this one is being watched from far beyond the country's boundaries.

Home to the largest section of the Amazon rainforest, Brazil is guardian of a carbon sink of vital importance, making the country's conservation policies globally relevant.

Environmentalists have already voiced alarm at Flávio Bolsonaro — son of right-wing former president Jair Bolsonaro — coming out 2% ahead of incumbent Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva in the first round of elections.

"It's going to be a second coming of Bolsonaro, therefore a more radical Bolsonaro," said Claudio Angelo, international policy coordinator at Climate Observatory, a network of Brazilian NGOs.

"At the risk of sounding alarmist, I would say Lula's re-election is the only thing standing between us and the end of the Amazon."

The Amazon rainforest stores huge amounts of the carbon dioxide (CO2) released into the atmosphere from the burning of fossil fuels. As a result, it helps regulate the Earth's climate.

For decades, the tropical forest has acted as one of nature's most powerful defenses against climate change. But it has also suffered from widespread deforestation.

During Jair Bolsonaro's presidency, from 2019 until 2023, environmental agencies were weakened and enforcement efforts curtailed, meaning tropical forest was cleared at unprecedented rates.

"The environment agenda was disregarded and the level of control the government had on pollution overall, but specifically on deforestation, was reversed," said Claudio Forner, head of climate policy at Berlin-based think tank Climate Analytics.

Over Bolsonaro's four-year term, an estimated 45,586km2 of the Amazon was destroyed, an area bigger than the size of Denmark. And the forest is not as resilient as it once was, with scientists warning that parts of it are now emitting more carbon than they absorb.

Lula came to office with a message of protection for the Amazon. Besides calling for an end to deforestation by 2030, he increased monitoring of the forest and strengthened Brazil's environmental enforcement agency IBAMA.

Deforestation has plummeted under his leadership, with 2026 set to be its lowest level in a decade. That represents a 41% drop overall compared to Jair Bolsonaro's tenure.

Experts have already criticized Flávio Bolsonaro's record on forest protection. As a senator in 2019, he authored draft legislation that could have stark impacts for the Amazon. While landowners are currently required to preserve 80% of their property for the growth of native plants, the bill would allow them to use their holdings as they please.

"The signal from the president will say 'now you can deforest' or 'it's harder to deforest,'" said Ana Yang, director of the Environment and Society Center at London-based think tank Chatham House.

"The moment that you decrease enforcement capability, deforestation grows," she told DW.

Bolsonaro has also pledged to end illegal deforestation by 2029, a year before Lula. But Claudio Angelo is critical of that message.

"He will end illegal deforestation not by enforcing the law, but by making it legal," he said.

More generally, environmental experts fear Bolsonaro might dismantle many of the protections strengthened under the current president.

"Unfortunately, climate change has become a partisan issue, so rather than offering a solution from their perspective, the right pictures climate change as an invented problem...that regulation goes against economic growth or it goes against competitiveness, that it's the aim of the left to control the means of production," Forner said.

In an interview with Brazilian media in August, Bolsonaro said he regarded extreme weather events to be cyclical and did not believe they were solely linked to human influence.

A Bolsonaro win is likely to see Brazil move away from its position as a climate leader, a role the country embraced last year as it hosted the COP30 climate conference in the Amazonian city of Belem.

But even a Lula victory would not represent a straightforward climate success story, say experts. While the current administration has overseen significant reductions in deforestation, it has also backed plans to expand oil production.

Angelo said the decision to allow exploratory drilling was a "betrayal" of Lula's mandate but added that the president's overall record on climate had been "quite impressive".

"Lula is the world's worst climate leader, except for all the others," he told DW.

The current president will face a runoff election with Bolsonaro on October 25. But while experts say the outcome could determine the fate of the Amazon and the wider efforts to curb global warming, for many Brazilians climate is not high on their list of priorities.

"Ultimately what people care about is access to education, sanitation and health," said Yang. A road built through the Amazon to access those services, while criticized from outside Brazil for destroying parts of the rainforest, would be desirable to voters, she added.

"They don't have time to care. Not because they don't want to. They care about their own survival," she said.

#755. Osaka's outfit dazzles again in Japan win as Williams loses to teenager

The Spaniard faces Jurij Rodionov or Nikoloz Basilashvili next.

Cameron Norrie also came through with a 6-4 6-1 victory over Francisco Cerundolo.

Tennis great Venus Williams fell to 0-13 in singles matches this year after losing 6-2 6-2 to teenager Lilli Tagger in the opening round of the Guangzhou Open.

The 28-year age gap between the two players is understood to be a record difference between opponents in a WTA Tour match, with Tagger born in 2008 - the year Williams won the last of her seven Grand Slam singles titles at Wimbledon.

Williams - now ranked No 599 and aged 46 - was playing her first singles match for two months since the US Open, where she lost in the first round to Sofia Kenin. Williams created no break-point chances and Tagger took four of the 16 she had.

"When I saw the draw I was super excited and a bit nervous - I respect her a lot," Tagger said. "It is funny she used to play with my coach [Francesca Schiavone]. I would love to play at this age - I want to congratulate her."

#754. A revolutionary new microscope could reveal the hidden machinery of life

Almost a century ago, a breakthrough in microscopy gave scientists an entirely new way to examine the inside of cells. Known as phase contrast, the technique made previously faint or nearly invisible cellular structures much easier to distinguish. Its importance was recognized with a Nobel Prize in 1953.

Now, physicists at the University of California, Berkeley, have brought that same principle to electron microscopy, a technology capable of magnifying objects roughly 10,000 times more than conventional light microscopes.

The researchers have developed a device called a laser phase plate that could significantly improve cryoelectron microscopy (cryo-EM). This powerful imaging method has transformed the study of protein structures and helped speed up drug discovery over the past decade. Even so, it has a major limitation: Most human proteins are so small that obtaining clear images of them remains extremely difficult.

The new laser technology could help overcome that obstacle, potentially allowing scientists to image proteins as small as one-third the size of those that challenge current microscopes.

An even greater impact could come from improvements to cryoelectron tomography (cryo-ET). This newer approach combines images captured from multiple angles to construct three-dimensional views of proteins and other molecules. Unlike techniques that study isolated molecules in a solution, cryo-ET can reveal how proteins are arranged and interact within their natural cellular surroundings.

“Cryo-EM has become the new, fastest-growing method for resolving the structure of biological macromolecules, and cryo-ET is expected to show how these molecules work together in their natural, cellular context,” said Holger Müller, a UC Berkeley professor of physics and faculty scientist at Lawrence Berkeley National Laboratory who led the development effort. “But because of signal-to-noise limitations, the majority of human and animal proteins are too small to be analyzed by these methods. The increase in signal-to-noise ratio provided by this laser phase plate is expected to overcome these important limitations.”

The breakthrough relies on what the researchers describe as the world's most intense focused continuous-wave laser. Rather than simply illuminating a specimen, the laser interacts directly with the microscope's electron beam, altering its phase to increase image contrast.

This adjustment makes it easier to distinguish small molecules, including hemoglobin, from their surroundings. It could also improve the visibility of structures inside cells, such as the nucleus and mitochondria.

“With cryo-ET, we're looking at small, very complicated cellular material that’s incredibly crowded inside the cell,” said Bridget Carragher, founding technical director of imaging at Biohub in Redwood City, California. “It’s like a forest of trees, and you’re trying to find one leaf on one tree in there. Cryo-ET needs a dramatic step forward in contrast, so we can start to see what's going on inside the cell. That's what the laser phase plate promises to give us.”

With financial support from Biohub, Müller acquired an advanced cryo-EM instrument and equipped it with the new laser phase plate. He named the resulting microscope Theia, after the ancient Greek Titaness associated with light and radiance.

Meanwhile, Carragher is directing the construction of a related microscope at Biohub's imaging facility in Redwood City. Her team's instrument uses two lasers rather than one, building on theoretical research conducted by Müller and his collaborators.

The two laser beams are arranged at right angles to one another and each operates at approximately half the power. This configuration helps prevent components from burning out while also reducing optical distortions that could interfere with image quality.

Both research teams are working with Thermo Fisher Scientific, the leading manufacturer of cryo-EM instruments.

“Theia is the Formula 1 microscope,” Müller said. “It has extra electron optics that give it better resolution than the standard cryo-EM, even without the laser. With the addition of the laser phase plate, we hope that it really becomes the world's best instrument overall.”

The Berkeley team's latest images and technical findings are presented in the journal Science. Details of Biohub's two-laser design are available in a recently posted preprint.

To understand why the new technology matters, it helps to look at a longstanding challenge in biological imaging.

Because plant and animal cells consist largely of water, they are relatively transparent when viewed under an ordinary light microscope. Although this transparency might seem beneficial, it creates a problem. Small cellular structures, including mitochondria and nuclei, scatter very little light and therefore appear only slightly darker than the surrounding material.

Scientists have traditionally addressed this problem by adding stains to create greater contrast. However, staining also kills the cells.

In 1930, Dutch physicist Frits Zernike realized that light passing through biological material changes in more ways than simply becoming brighter or dimmer. When light scatters inside a cell, it also experiences a tiny delay that changes its phase, or the timing of its wave peaks.

Although the human eye cannot directly detect this phase shift, Zernike discovered that it could be converted into a visible difference in brightness. By shifting the phase of the unscattered light by 90 degrees, he made subtle cellular features stand out more clearly against their background when the light reached the viewer's eye.

This innovation became the foundation of phase-contrast microscopy, earning Zernike the 1953 Nobel Prize in Physics.

By the early 1940s, scientists had already recognized how useful phase contrast was for studying cells. They began exploring whether the same idea might improve electron microscopes, which use electrons instead of light to examine structures as small as individual proteins.

Unfortunately, early attempts encountered serious technical difficulties. Devices designed to alter the phase of electron beams often weakened the beam too much, introduced image instability, or reduced resolution.

A possible solution emerged in 2010, when Müller and Robert Glaeser, now a UC Berkeley professor emeritus of molecular and cell biology, proposed using an intense laser to produce the necessary phase shift. Their approach offered a way to improve contrast without sacrificing electron beam intensity.

Glaeser has played an important role in developing cryo-EM, which offers a potentially simpler way to determine molecular structures than traditional X-ray crystallography.

X-ray crystallography requires researchers to grow crystals of the molecules they want to study and obtain access to an intense X-ray source. Cryo-EM avoids the need to crystallize molecules, but it presents difficulties of its own.

One of the biggest problems is that an electron beam can heat and eventually damage the specimen being examined. This limits how much information can be collected and how detailed the resulting images can become. Covering specimens with metal can reduce damage and improve contrast, but it also makes the images less sharp.

During the 1960s, researchers began investigating whether freezing biological samples could protect them from radiation damage.

Glaeser demonstrated that cooling specimens helped preserve them during imaging. He also proposed using a less intense electron beam to examine thousands of frozen molecules at once, further limiting the damage.

Because the molecules would be positioned at different angles, computers could analyze and combine their images to reconstruct a highly detailed molecular structure.

These advances helped establish the foundations of modern cryo-EM, whose developers received the 2017 Nobel Prize in Chemistry. During their acceptance remarks, the Nobel laureates acknowledged Glaeser's contributions.

The Nobel Committee praised the technology, explaining that cryo-EM “both simplifies and improves the imaging of biomolecules. This method has moved biochemistry into a new era.”

Turning the laser phase plate concept into working technology required years of engineering.

Following their 2010 proposal, Müller spent 15 years pursuing the idea, with part of the research supported by the National Institutes of Health.

The first major challenge was generating an extraordinarily intense laser beam concentrated into a tiny area. The researchers needed enough light intensity to change the phase of an electron beam by 90 degrees.

It took a decade to develop the necessary system.

Their solution was to confine a continuous laser beam inside a spherical chamber lined with mirrors. As the light repeatedly reflected through the cavity more than 10,000 times, its intensity increased dramatically while the beam became focused into an extremely small spot.

“It’s 75 kilowatts focused to a few microns,” Müller said. “That’s more powerful than what you use for welding. It's more power than a military laser. It builds up the brightest continuous laser focus ever.”

The team first demonstrated that the concept worked by installing a laser phase plate in one of Glaeser's older electron microscopes.

Additional funding from Biohub then made it possible to acquire a customized, state-of-the-art Thermo Fisher Krios cryo-EM microscope and modify it to accommodate the laser technology.

In their latest research, the scientists tested the upgraded microscope using six different samples that varied in size and preparation method. The laser consistently improved image resolution, with particularly important gains for specimens that are difficult to image using standard methods.

“For the most challenging cases — small particles, bad specimens — the laser produces a very considerable advantage,” Müller said.

To demonstrate the new microscope's capabilities, the researchers reconstructed images of two different proteins.

The first was aldolase, a protein found in muscle that modern cryo-EM systems can already image relatively easily. The second was hemoglobin, the protein responsible for carrying oxygen through the bloodstream.

Hemoglobin is much smaller and lies near the lower size limit of proteins that conventional cryo-EM instruments can reliably analyze.

Using the laser phase plate, the researchers improved the resolution of both protein structures. The improvement was especially pronounced for hemoglobin, highlighting the technology's potential to overcome the difficulties associated with smaller molecules.

“The bottom line is, if you have a large protein and a really good sample — a fresh one or one frozen without bubbles, for example — you may not need the phase plate to get a single, high-quality image. But for a small protein and a bad sample, laser-on is best,” Müller said. “This could fill an enormous gap in our knowledge of protein structures that can’t be crystallized or are too small for today’s cryo-EM. And it will be revolutionary for cryo-ET.”

Protein mass is typically expressed in daltons, a unit named after English chemist John Dalton. One dalton is defined as one-twelfth the mass of a carbon-12 atom.

Conventional cryo-EM struggles to image proteins smaller than approximately 70 kilodaltons. That limitation is particularly significant because proteins below this threshold account for about 90% of the human proteome, the complete collection of proteins produced by the human body.

With the laser phase plate, researchers can now image proteins as small as 50 kilodaltons, although doing so remains challenging. Such proteins are even smaller than hemoglobin.

Müller believes the technology could eventually allow scientists to study proteins as small as 17 kilodaltons (the size of the protein myoglobin).

Reaching that goal may require another improvement: using a tightly focused electron beam rather than the defocused beam currently needed to produce sufficient contrast without the laser phase plate.

According to Müller, the laser system should make it possible to obtain contrast directly from phase changes in a focused electron beam.

If successful, this adjustment could double the contrast and signal-to-noise ratio again, adding to the improvement already achieved with the laser phase plate.

Such progress could make it possible to study a far larger proportion of the body's proteins and provide unprecedented insight into how they interact within the complex environment of cells.

“This technology is a step function change for biology,” said Stephani Otte, Biohub’s Vice President of Imaging Science. “We are going to be able to see how molecular machines operate inside the living cell, in context, for the first time. What was once invisible will become visible — and that changes everything about how we understand disease.”

The Berkeley research team also includes Glaeser; postdoctoral fellows and co-first authors Petar Petrov and Jessie Zhang; staff scientist Jonathan Remis; postdoctoral fellow Hang Cheng; and current and former physics graduate students Jeremy Axelrod, Eric Cooper, Ian Hicklin, Shahar Sandhaus, and Cooper Schnurr.

At Biohub, Carragher and David Agard, the organization's founding scientific director of imaging, jointly lead the Dynamic Structural Cell Biology group. Both are co-corresponding authors of the Biohub preprint, alongside Biohub engineer Pavel Olshin.

Materials provided by University of California - Berkeley. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#751. Apple officially rolls out iOS 27.2. Heres whats new

Apple has a fresh new iOS update for everyone to check out.

Monday marked the official launch of iOS 27.2, which is now available as a software update on all compatible iPhones. To install it, simply open the Settings app, tap General, and then tap Software Update. No need to fiddle with beta software this time — this is the real deal.

As for what's new, iOS 27.2 isn't the most monumental update in the world, but there's plenty to mess around with. For starters, Siri AI is now available in several new languages: French, Japanese, Korean, Portuguese, and Spanish. It's also been updated for English-language users so it will now be able to recall past conversations you've had with Apple's chatbot. Additionally, the Messages app will offer context-sensitive suggestions to take quick actions in Calendar or Reminders based on what's being said in the text conversation.

Dual Capture now works on FaceTime group calls, meaning you can have both your front and rear cameras active at the same time while talking to more than one other person. For new iPhone Duo owners, iOS 27.2 also adds Apple Pencil support. Meanwhile, the Edge Light feature uses the iPhone Duo's display to light up your face during video calls in low light.

There are plenty of other small changes to check out, too, such as individual profiles in the Apple TV app and a reimagined Up Next view in the Podcasts app. Multiview will also let sports fans watch up to four events simultaneously, though the feature appears to be limited to sporting events on Apple TV.

In total, iOS 27.2 might not be the most seismic update in the world, but there's still a good amount to play with.

Alex Perry is a tech reporter at Mashable who primarily covers video games and consumer tech. Alex has spent most of the last decade reviewing games, smartphones, headphones, and laptops, and he doesn’t plan on stopping anytime soon. He is also a Pisces, a cat lover, and a Kansas City sports fan. Alex can be found on Bluesky at yelix.bsky.social.

#750. Musk's Twitter promised a purge of blue check marks. Instead he singled out the New York Times

Some VIP Twitter users woke up on Saturday expecting to have lost their coveted blue verification check marks in a previously announced purge by Elon Musk. Instead, Twitter appeared to target a single account from a major publication Musk dislikes and changed the language on its site in a way that obscures why users are verified.

Twitter had said it would “begin winding down” blue checks granted under its old verification system — which emphasized protecting high-profile users at risk of impersonation — on April 1. In order to stay verified, Musk said, users would have to pay $8 per month to join the platform’s Twitter Blue subscription service, which has allowed accounts to pay for verification since December.

But one high-profile account did lose its blue check over the weekend: the main account for the New York Times, which had previously told CNN it would not pay for verification.

William Shatner is mad he may have to pay to keep his blue Twitter checkmark. Elon Musk says too bad

After an account that often engages with Musk posted a meme this weekend about the Times declining to pay for verification, Musk responded in a tweet saying, “Oh ok, we’ll take it off then.” Musk then lashed out at the Times — just the latest instance of the billionaire slamming journalists or media outlets — in a series of tweets that claimed the outlet’s coverage is boring and “propaganda.”

The weekend moves are just the latest example of Twitter creating confusion and whiplash for users over feature changes — and in this case, not just any users, but many of the most high-profile accounts that have long been a key selling point for the platform. It also highlights how Musk often appears to guide decisions about the platform more by whims than by policy.

Although the New York Times’ main account lost its blue check, its other accounts, such as those for its arts, travel and books content, remained verified. (It’s not clear why the New York Times doesn’t have a gold “organizations” check mark, like accounts for other news outlets, including the Associated Press and the Washington Post.) After its blue check was removed, a spokesperson for the New York Times reiterated to CNN that it does not plan to pay for verification.

Twitter, which laid off most of its public relations staff last fall, did not immediately respond to a request for comment.

In a separate puzzling move, Twitter’s blue bird logo at the top of the site was replaced on Monday with doge, the meme representing the cryptocurrency dogecoin, which Musk has promoted. The price of dogecoin shot up 20% on Monday.

Musk has been threatening to take away “legacy” blue check marks from users verified under Twitter’s old system since shortly after he bought Twitter last fall.

In early November, Twitter launched the option for people paying for its Twitter Blue subscription service to receive blue checks. The program was quickly put on pause after being plagued by a wave of celebrity and corporate impersonators, and was relaunched in December.

Twitter also rolled out a color-coded verification system with differently colored marks for companies and government entities, but Musk continued to say that individual users would eventually have to pay for blue checks.

In the days leading up to the blue check purge that wasn’t, prominent users such as actor William Shatner and anti-bullying activist Monica Lewinksy pushed back against the idea that, as power users that draw attention to the site, they should have to pay for a feature that keeps them safe from impersonation.

By muddying the reason accounts are verified, the new label could risk making it easier for people to scam or impersonate high-profile users. Experts in inauthentic behavior have also said it’s not clear that reserving verification for paid users will reduce the number of bots on the site, an issue Musk has raised on and off over the past year.

Musk, for his part, has previously presented changes to Twitter’s verification system as a way of “treating everyone equally.”

“There shouldn’t be a different standard for celebrities,” he said in a tweet last week. The paid feature could also drive revenue, which could help Musk, who is on the hook for significant debt after buying Twitter for $44 billion.

Musk last week also said that starting on April 15, only verified accounts would be recommended in users’ “For You” feeds alongside the accounts they follow.

–CNN’s Oliver Darcy contributed to this report.
